Pseudolimits for Tangent Categories and Equivariant Tangents for Varieties and Smooth Manifolds
Dorette Pronk, Geoff Vooys

Abstract
. In this paper we show that if 𝒞 \mathscr{C} is a category and if F : 𝒞 → ℭ ​ 𝔞 ​ 𝔱 F\colon\mathscr{C}\to\mathfrak{Cat} is a pseudofunctor such that for each object X X of 𝒞 \mathscr{C} the category F ⁡ ( X ) F(X) is a tangent category and for each morphism f f of 𝒞 \mathscr{C} the functor F ⁡ ( f ) F(f) is part of a strong tangent morphism ( F ( f ) , f α ) (F(f),\!\,_{f}{\alpha}) and that furthermore the natural transformations α f \!\,{}_{f}{\alpha} vary pseudonaturally in 𝒞 op \mathscr{C}^{\operatorname{op}} , then there is a tangent structure on the pseudolimit 𝐏𝐂 ⁡ ( F ) \mathbf{PC}(F) which is induced by the tangent structures on the categories F ⁡ ( X ) F(X) together with how they vary through the functors F ⁡ ( f ) F(f) . We use this observation to show that the forgetful 2 2 -functor Forget : 𝔗 ​ 𝔞 ​ 𝔫 → ℭ ​ 𝔞 ​ 𝔱 \operatorname{Forget}:\mathfrak{Tan}\to\mathfrak{Cat} creates and preserves pseudolimits indexed by 1 1 -categories. As an application, this allows us to describe how equivariant descent interacts with the tangent structures on the category of smooth (real) manifolds and on various categories of (algebraic) varieties over a field.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 18F40; Secondary 18F20, 14A99, 53C10

1.
Introduction

Tangent categories describe a categorification of differential geometry by focusing on and abstracting the properties of the tangent bundle functor
T\colon\mathbf{SMan}\to\mathbf{SMan}
, where
\mathbf{SMan}
is the category of smooth (real) manifolds, and formalizing notions of tangent bundle and differential bundle that can be applied in further abstract categorical settings.
This abstract formalism provides an algebraic description of what it means to be smooth and brings monadic techniques to the study of manifolds and differential-geometric objects.
The notion of tangent categories was originally developed in
[
22
]
and then rediscovered and generalized in
[
2
]
.
Tangent categories are now an area of study in category theory in their own right and they are used to study problems in differential geometry, algebraic geometry, linear logic, the
\lambda
-calculus, machine learning, synthetic differential geometry, programming theory, and more.
However, while tangent categories do a good job of creating an algebraic/equational formulation of what it means for a (real) manifold to be smooth, we have not yet extended this formalism to model interactions with group actions and local symmetries.
More generally, we still need to study the ways in which tangent structures interact with equivariant descent. A central ingredient towards studying these interactions lies in understanding pseudolimits of diagrams in the
2
-category
\fTan
of tangent categories.

Pseudolimits are a higher-categorical weakening of the notion of a limit in traditional category theory where the triangles that define the cones involving the projections are only required to commute up to coherent isomorphisms. Pseudolimits provide important tools in higher categorical geometry and topology and in particular in descent theory, as they provide a structure with which to track the ways in which you recognize objects to be “sufficiently equivalent” to each other. In fact, the categories of equivariant sheaves and the equivariant derived categories which appear in algebraic geometry, algebraic topology, differential geometry, and representation theory (when one wants to know about equivariant cohomology) can all be viewed as pseudolimits. This perspective is explored at great length in
[
25
]
and used to study both equivariant algebraic geometry and equivariant algebraic topology.
In this paper we will explore how pseudolimits facilitate equivariance for tangent structures in categorical differential geometry.

Because of their importance as a technical tool in studying equivariant descent and tangent theory simultaneously, the first two-thirds of the paper focuses on the nature of when the pseudolimits of pseudofunctors
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
(for
\Cscr
a
1
-category) are tangent categories based on the structure of the fibre categories
F(X)
for objects
X
of
\Cscr
and based on the nature of the transition functors
F(f)
for morphisms
f
of
\Cscr
. This then leads us to study how the hom-
2
-category
\mathsf{Bicat}(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
can be used to probe the
2
-category
\fTan
of tangent categories for certain pseudolimits.
We then show that, under the same mild technical assumptions as just described, the pseudolimit in
\fCat
of a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
, when equipped with its pseudolimit tangent structure (defined and proved to exist below), is actually a pseudolimit of the corresponding diagram in
\fCat
(in other words, the forgetful
2
-functor
\Forget:\fTan\to\fCat
preserves and reflects pseudolimits of pseudofunctors
F
whose domain is a
1
-category). Because these calculations show the structure of how tangent structures must behave with respect to descent, we then use our study of pseudolimits and tangent categories to study equivariant tangent structures in algebraic and differential geometry.

The study of equivariance (as the broad interpretation of how functions and maps may record and witness group actions) and equivariant objects in mathematics is deep and varied. These objects arise in the study of representation theory, algebraic geometry, algebraic topology, homotopy theory, statistics, number theory, analysis, stack theory, descent theory, and everywhere and anywhere in between. Of particular interest in equivariant mathematics are orbifolds and geometric representation theory. In both areas of research we want to work with generalized quotients because real quotients do not have the correct structure (for instance, they may not be smooth) and lose too much information. The notion of orbifold atlas handles the problem by keeping track of the local (finite) isotropy groups with their actions on charts and the ways these actions are related to each other, while the Langlands Programme uses descent data taken in quotients of actions of algebraic groups on sufficiently nice varieties in order to study representations of groups in geometric and ring-theoretic ways.
Orbifolds may also be represented by a particular type of smooth groupoids; in this case the group actions are encoded in the groupoid structure. We see that in all these cases there are natural notions of tangents, but describing these tangent structures formally requires studying how the equivariant descent and tangent theories interact.

In this paper we will show how to make sense of having a descent-equivariant tangent structure on varieties over fields and on smooth manifolds with an action of a compact Lie group.
This gives an idea of how to get tangent category theory to interact with the language of descent theory, scheme theory, differential geometry, and group representation theory. Applications to orbifolds, however, will be presented in a sequel paper.

Finally, some words regarding the general nature of this paper. In
[
17
]
, Leung showed that to give a tangent category structure on a category
\Cscr
is the same as giving the category
\Cscr
a
\mathbf{Weil}_{1}
-actegory
1
1

1

The category
\mathbf{Weil}_{1}
is the category of Weil-algebras which is a subcategory of the category of augmented nilpotent
\N
-algebras; cf.
[
17
]
or
[
19
]
for details.
structure. A more high-level approach to what we are doing in this paper could be taken by constructing a certain
\mathbf{Weil}_{1}
-actegorical structure on the pseudolimit; however, we do not take this perspective in this paper. Instead, we take a much more explicit and calculation-based approach. Because of the applications to scheme theory, orbifold theory, and representation theory we anticipate it is important to have tools and examples showing exactly how computations and manipulations involving equivariant categories should be executed. Additionally, when seeking how to have descent theory (at least as far as it is recorded by a pseudofunctor) and tangent-theoretic information interact we anticipate that it will be useful to have these computations worked out and presented for future work and future practitioners.

It is also worth noting that Lanfranchi has introduced the notion of what it means to be a tangent object (as well as a morphism of tangent objects and a
2
-morphism between morphisms of tangent objects) internal to a
2
-category in
[
16
]
. We use this perspective frequently and seriously in the first two-thirds of the paper in order to show how tangent objects in the
2
-category
\mathsf{Bicat}(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
compare with various concepts in
[
16
]
, as well as how they can be used to determine the pseudolimits in the
2
-category
\fTan
of tangent categories which are taken over diagrams
D
whose domain is a
1
-category.

1.1.
Structure of the Paper

The structure of the paper is as follows. In Section
2
we review the basic theory of pseudocones, as they form a central technical backbone of the paper. We also introduce the category
\mathbf{PC}(F)
of pseudocones of a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
in this section. As shown in
[
25
]
, this gives a pseudolimit for
F
in
\fCat
. We also discuss why the lax version of this construction is more poorly behaved and why this necessitates working with pseudonatural transformations and not just lax or oplax transformations in this paper. Additionally, we give a detailed study of the limits which appear in the psuedocone categories
\mathbf{PC}(F)
.

In Section
3
we review the basic theory of tangent categories as developed by Cockett and Cruttwell before taking the rest of the section to prove the first result main results of the paper as expressed in the following two theorems. Both these theorems rely on our first key definition: that of a tangent indexing functor (cf. Definition
3.8
).

Theorem 1.1

(cf. Theorem
3.33
)
.

Let
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a tangent indexing functor. Then the category
\mathbf{PC}(F)
is a tangent category.

Theorem 1.2

(cf. Theorem
3.41
)
.

Let
\Cscr
be a
1
-category and consider the
2
-category
\mathsf{Bicat}(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
. Then a tuple
(F,T,p,\operatorname{add},0,\ell,c)
in
\mathsf{Bicat}(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
is a tangent object in the sense of
[
16
, Definition 3.2]
if and only if
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is a tangent indexing functor.

Note that we give a rather detailed explicit proof of these theorems, as we want to provide the details one needs in order to be able to perform computations with equivariant categories and the descent-equivariant tangent structures (cf. Sections
5
–
7
).

In Section
4
we prove that the pseudolimit tangent categories are suitably functorial. In particular, after recalling what it means to have morphisms between tangent objects and transformations between tangent morphisms in a general
2
-category, we characterize the morphisms and transformations in the
2
-category
\fTan(\mathsf{Bicat}(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat))
. We then use this to prove the next main theorems of the paper which show that the category
\fTan
has all pseudolimits indexed by
1
-categorical diagrams and that the forgetful
2
-functor
\fTan\to\fCat
reflects and preserves these pseudolimits.

Theorem 1.3

(cf. Theorem
4.9
)
.

The strict
2
-functor
\mathbf{PC}(-)
restricts to a strict
2
-functor

\mathbf{PC}\colon\mathfrak{Tan}\left(\mathsf{Bicat}\left(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat\right)\right)\to\fTan

and makes the diagram of
2
-categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Bicat}\left(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fTan\left(\mathsf{Bicat}\left(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fTan}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PC}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PC}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}

commute strictly.

Theorem 1.4

(cf. Theorem
4.10
)
.

Let
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a tangent indexing functor. The tangent category
\mathbf{PC}(F)
equipped with the tangent structure of Theorem
3.33
is the pseudolimit in
\fTan
of shape
F
.

In Section
5
we review and recall the foundations towards doing equivariant algebraic geometry and equivariant differential geometry. Note that there are many technical footnotes in this section for the reader who is interested in some of the technical details in equivariant geometry, but these details can be safely skipped for the reader who is willing to go with the adjective-laden flow. Most of what appears here is located in various locations in the literature, but we collect the results centrally here to introduce the reader to how to go about the equivariant geometry yoga and to also illustrate the remarkable similarities between the algebraic and differential cases. We also present twelve different examples of the sorts of “equivariant categories” which can appear over/on varities
X
and smooth manifolds
M
in Example
5.19
.

In Section
6
we compute an extended example and construct the descent equivariant tangent category of schemes over a fixed variety
X
. To do this we recall the Zariski tangent structure on
\Sch_{/S}
for an arbitrary scheme
S
. After doing this we prove that for an arbitrary scheme morphism
f\colon S\to T
,
f^{\ast}\colon\Sch_{/T}\to\Sch_{/S}
is a strong tangent morphism for the Zariski tangent structure and that these tangent morphisms vary pseudofunctorially in
S
and
T
. Putting these together we show how to define the tangent category of descent equivariant schemes over a variety
X
; cf. Definition
6.16
and Theorem
6.17
for details.

In Section
7
we do a second extended example and show how to build the descent equivariant tangent category of smooth manifolds over a fixed smooth manifold
M
equipped with a (smooth) action by a Lie group
L
by defining a tangent indexing functor. To do this we recall the notion of what it means to be an étale map in a tangent category and then prove some small results about how étale maps in tangent categories interact with pullback functors between slice tangent structures. Afterwards we define our pseudofunctor and prove that it is a tangent indexing functor; taking the pseudolimit of this diagram then gives us the descent equivariant tangent category of smooth manifolds over
M
.

Finally, in Section
8
we make some comments towards generalizing the results in this paper. In particular, we discuss some thoughts towards generalizing our constructions to orbifolds.

Throughout the paper we will keep careful track of the role of the structure 2-cells in the whole framework; e.g., although the pseudolimit of a diagram defined by a pseudofunctor is functorial along pseudonatural transformations, it need not be along lax or oplax transformations. It is tempting to want to generalize our work to lax indexing functors, (op)lax limits and the category of tangent categories with lax or colax morphisms. However, this is not possible and there are several reasons for this. To clarify what can be generalized and what cannot be generalized, we will add comments throughout the paper to make the reader aware of these 2-categorical subtleties.

1.2.
Remarks on Chronology and Perspectives

The key definition we use in this paper, namely that of a tangent indexing functor (cf. Definition
3.8
), is similar to what is called an indexed tangent category in
[
16
, Definition 3.7]
. On the face, our notions are simply similar: on one hand, we define tangent indexing functors to be pseudofunctors
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
which, broadly speaking, have categories
F(X)
given by tangent categories and which have functors
F(f)
which form the functor component of a morphism
(F(f),\!\,_{f}{\alpha})
of a strong morphism of tangent categories (in the lax direction) for which the transformations
\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha}
vary pseudonaturally in
\Cscr^{\op}
(cf. Definition
3.3
for the definition of strong tangent morphisms). On the other hand, in
[
16
]
an indexed tangent category is a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
equipped with a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fTan_{\operatorname{strong}}
where
\fTan_{\operatorname{strong}}
is the
2
-category of tangent categories and strong tangent morphisms. These notions are readily compared and contrasted in the following immediate ways:

•

The definition of an indexed tangent category
((\Cscr,\Tbb),F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fTan_{\operatorname{strong}})
in
[
16
]
has nothing to do with the tangent structure
\Tbb
on
\Cscr
. For instance, if
\Ibb
is the identity tangent structure and if
\Tbb
is an arbitrary tangent structure on
\Cscr
then
((\Cscr,\Ibb),F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fTan_{\operatorname{strong}})
and
((\Cscr,\Tbb),F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fTan_{\operatorname{strong}})
form indexed tangent categories with the same pseudofunctor and cannot be distinguished.

•

The definition of a tangent indexing functor (cf. Definition
3.8
), while presented in a minimal way, is equivalent to giving a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fTan_{\operatorname{strong}}
. The difference in perspective largely comes from the fact that we are interested primarily in pseudolimits, while
[
16
]
is interested in a tangent-categorical version of the Grothendieck construction.

We show that these two seemingly disctinct concepts are equivalent (cf. Theorems
3.40
and
3.41
) in the sense that both present data which is both necessary and sufficient to giving tangent objects in the
2
-category
\mathsf{Bicat}(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
. We present our focus on indexing tangent functors in order to both be in a position to ask for minimal structure and prove what exists by virtue of the pseudofunctor itself, as well as to show that the
2
-functor
\Forget:\fTan\to\fCat
creates pseudolimits of pseudofunctors
F
which are indexed by
1
-categories. Finally, we would also like to remark that a preliminary version of our notion of tangent indexing functor appeared prior to
[
16
]
: the second author first presented a notion of tangent indexing functor (and in particular the notions which appear in Sections
5
and
6
regarding varieties) at the 2022 Foundational Methods in Computer Science conference in
[
24
]
.

2.
A Review of Pseudocones

In this section we review the 2-categorical material on pseudolimits that will be used as foundation for everything we do in this paper.
Specifically, we will be considering lax and pseudo cones over pseudofunctors into
\fCat
, the 2-category of categories.
For readers new to the theory of 2-categories and bicategories, we recommend
[
13
]
as a reference.
Specifically, the definitions of 2-categories and bicategories can be found in
[
13
, Definition 2.1.3]
. The notion of pseudofunctor is defined in
[
13
, Definition 4.1.2]
. However, as we will use slightly different notation for the laxity constraints, also called structure cells, of a lax or pseudofunctor we include our version of the definition of pseudofunctor here:

Definition 2.1
.

Given two bicategories
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, a pseudofunctor
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
consists of

•

A function
F_{0}\colon\mathcal{B}_{0}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
on objects;

•

For each pair of objects
X,Y\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
, a local functor

F_{A,B}\colon\mathcal{B}(X,Y)\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(F_{0}X,F_{0}Y)

•

For each triple of objects
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{B}
, invertible structure transformations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bcal(Y,Z)\times\Bcal(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bcal(X,Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bcal^{\prime}(FY,FZ)\times\Bcal^{\prime}(FX,FY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bcal^{\prime}(FX,FZ)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{Y,Z}\times F_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X,Y,Z}{\phi}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bcal(X,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bcal^{\prime}(FX,FX)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{\prime}_{FX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X,X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\phi}}

with component 2-cells
\phi_{g,f}={\!\,{}_{X,Y,Z}{\phi}}_{g,f}\colon Fg\circ Ff\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}F(gf)
and
\phi_{X}={\!\,{}_{X}{\phi}}_{*}\colon 1^{\prime}_{FX}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}F(1_{X})
, where
*
is the unique object of
\mathbf{1}
. (Note that we will generally not use the more cumbersome notation involving the objects.)
These 2-cells are required to satisfy coherence conditions with respect to the associativity and unity cells of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
as described in
[
13
, Definition 4.1.2]
.

The notions of lax natural transformation between these functors is described in
[
13
, Defintion 4.2.1]
and the modifications between transformations can be found in
[
13
, Definition 4.4.1]
.
Here we just want to point out that such a transformation
\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G\colon\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is given by the following data:

•

for each object
X\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
, an arrow
\alpha_{X}\colon FX\to GX
in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
;

•

for each arrow
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}Y
in
\mathcal{B}
, a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f}}

These data need to satisfy unity and naturality conditions as can be found in
[
13
, Definition 4.4.1]
.

Remark 2.2
.

We make the simplifying assumption that all pseudofunctors
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
we consider in this paper are normalized, i.e.,
F(\id_{A})=\id_{FA}
, for all objects
A\in\Cscr_{0}
, instead of merely having a natural isomorphism between the functors. This may be done without loss of generality; cf.
[
9
, Exposé VI.9, pg. 180, 181]
.

With this notation in place we can begin our discussion of pseudocones and pseudolimits.

2.1.
Pseudocones

Given a category
\Cscr
, a
lax cone

(\Kscr,\kappa)
with vertex
\Kscr
for a contravariant normal pseudofunctor
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is given by a lax transformation
\kappa\colon\const(\Kscr)\Rightarrow F
, where
\const(\Kscr)\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is the constant functor with value
\Kscr
on the objects and sending all arrows and 2-cells to identities.

In detail, this lax cone is given by a functor
\!\,{}_{C}{\kappa}\colon\Kscr\to FC
for each object
C\in\Cscr
and a natural transformation,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Kscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{D}{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{C}{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{f}{\kappa}}

for each arrow
D\xrightarrow{f}C
in
\Cscr
.
These transformations are functorial in the sense that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Kscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{C}{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{D}{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{E}{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{f}{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{g}{\kappa}}

is equal to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Kscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{E}{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{C}{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f\circ g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{g,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{g\circ f}{\kappa}}

(where
\phi_{g,f}
is a structure cell for the pseudofunctor
F
)
and
\!\,{}_{\id_{C}}{\kappa}=\id_{\!\,{}_{C}{\kappa}}
. The lax cone
(\Kscr,\kappa)
is a
pseudocone
if all
\!\,{}_{f}{\kappa}
are invertible 2-cells.

Remark 2.3
.

Note that we normally write the indices for the components of a transformation as right-hand indices. However, we will encounter a number of occasions, where the components of a transformation are themselves transformations or modifications that have components of their own. In that case, we will write the first components as left-indices, so that the right index is free for the next level.

Definition 2.4
.

A pseudocone
(\Lscr,\lambda)
over
F
is a
limiting pseudocone
if it satisfies the following two universal properties:

•

For any other pseudocone
(\Kscr,\kappa)
over
F
there is a unique arrow
r\colon\Kscr\to\Lscr
such that
\lambda_{C}r=\kappa_{C}
for all objects
C\in\Cscr
and
\lambda_{f}\ast r=\kappa_{f}
for each arrow
f
in
\Cscr
.

•

For any map between cones with the same vertex,
(\Kscr,\kappa)\Rightarrow(\Kscr,\kappa^{\prime})
given by a compatible family of 2-cells
\theta_{C}\colon\!\,_{C}{\kappa}\Rightarrow\!\,_{C}{\kappa^{\prime}}
there is a unique 2-cell
\rho\colon r\Rightarrow r^{\prime}\colon\Kscr\rightarrow\Lscr
between the induced unique arrows such that

\lambda_{C}\ast\rho=\theta_{C}
for all
C\in\Cscr
.

Remark 2.5
.

The basics of discussing limiting pseudocones were discussed in
[
8
, Chapitre I, Section 1.1.5]
originally under the language of fibred categories (without proof that their model gives a pseudolimit and without the pseudouniversal property) and also, more explicitly and
2
-categorically, in
[
15
, Section 6.10]
. We follow the exposition of
[
25
]
, however, as it is quite explicit and provides a concrete model for working with the pseudolimit of a pseudofunctor in
\fCat
.

By Theorem 2.3.16 on page 43 of
[
25
]
the apex for the limiting pseudocone for a pseudofunctor
F
into
\fCat
is given by

\mathbf{PC}(F)=\mathsf{Bicat}(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)(\const(\mathbbm{1}),F).

This is the category of pseudocones over
F
; it has been described in full detail in
[
25
]
. Here we include a quick description of its objects and arrows (taken from page 35 of
[
25
]
):

•

objects are natural transformations
(\mathbbm{1})\Rightarrow F
, which correspond to a family of objects,

A=\{\negthinspace\,_{X}{A}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0},\negthinspace\,_{X}{A}\in F(X)_{0}\}

with a family of invertible transition morphisms

\Sigma_{A}=\{\tau_{f}^{A}\colon F(f)(\negthinspace\,_{Y}{A})\xrightarrow{\sim}\negthinspace\,_{X}{A}\;|\;f\colon X\to Y,f\in\Cscr_{1}\}

satisfying the cocycle condition,

\tau_{g\circ f}^{A}\circ\phi_{f,g}=\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\left(\tau_{g}^{A}\right)

for any pair of composable morphisms
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
in
\Cscr
.
We will write
(A,\Sigma_{A})
for the object corresponding to this data.

•

a morphism
P\colon(A,\Sigma_{A})\to(B,\Sigma_{B})
is a modification determined by a collection of morphisms

P=\{\negthinspace\,_{X}{\rho}\colon\negthinspace\,_{X}{A}\to\negthinspace\,_{X}{B}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

such that for any morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\negthinspace\,_{X}{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\negthinspace\,_{Y}{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\negthinspace\,{}_{X}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\negthinspace\,{}_{X}{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\negthinspace\,_{X}{\rho})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho}}

commutes.

As described in
[
25
]
, the pseudonatural transformation
\const(\mathbf{PC}(F))\Rightarrow F
defining the limiting pseudocone has component functors
p_{X}\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to F(X)
, indexed by
X\in\Cscr_{0}
, defined as the composites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PC}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}

where
\pi_{X}
is the usual projection out of the product and
p\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to\prod_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)
is the functor which on objects
(A,T_{A})
forgets the transition isomorphisms and sends
A
to
(\negthinspace\,_{X}{A})_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}
and on morphisms sends
P
to
(\negthinspace\,_{X}{\rho})_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}
. Now define the natural isomorphism
p_{f}\colon F(f)\circ p_{Y}\Rightarrow p_{X}
by setting, for all objects
(A,T_{A})\in\mathbf{PC}(F)_{0}
,

p_{f}^{A}:=\tau_{f}^{A}.

2.2.
Functoriality of
\mathbf{PC}(F)

Similar to the case for ordinary cones for diagrams in 1-categories, a pseudonatural transformation
{\alpha}\colon F\Rightarrow E
gives rise to a morphism
\underline{\alpha}\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to\mathbf{PC}(E)
between their pseudocone categories, through composition,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Similarly, a modification
\rho\colon\alpha\Rrightarrow\beta\colon F\Rightarrow E
gives rise to a natural transformation

\underline{\rho}
between functors
\underline{\alpha},\underline{\beta}\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to\mathbf{PC}(E)
through pre-whiskering,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

However, in the next section we will need the exact details of the components of these morphisms and 2-cells; in particular, the explicit formulation of the transition isomorphisms of an object
\underline{\alpha}(A)
, in order to define tangent structures on pseudocones. We will give this explicit formulation in the next theorem, which was taken from
[
25
]
, and include parts of the proof, because it illustrates why we need to work with pseudofunctors in order for the category of pseudocones to have the universal property of the pseudolimit (and hence also the correct functoriality). In particular we show that this formulation of the transition isomorphisms cannot necessarily be done with lax or oplax transformations. For further explicit details regarding these calculations, see
[
25
, Theorem 4.1.1, Page 80]
for the case of the functors
\underline{\alpha}\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to\mathbf{PC}(E)
and
[
25
, Lemma 4.1.11, Page 90]
for the calculation of the explicit form of the natural transformations.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
25
, Theorem 4.1.1, Lemma 4.1.11]
)
.

If
F,E\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
are pseudofunctors and if
\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow E
is a pseudonatural transformation with component functors
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
and natural isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\circ F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\circ\,_{Y}{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

then there is a functor
\underline{\alpha}\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to\mathbf{PC}(E)
given on objects
(A,\Sigma_{A})
of
\mathbf{PC}(F)
by
\underline{\alpha}((A,\Sigma_{A})=(\underline{\alpha}(A),\Sigma_{\underline{\alpha}A})
, where

\underline{\alpha}(A)=\left\{\negthinspace\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\negthinspace\,_{X}{A})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0},\negthinspace\,_{X}{A}\in A\right\}

and

\Sigma_{\underline{\alpha}A}=\left\{\!\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)\circ\left(\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha}\right)_{\negthinspace\,{}_{Y}{A}}\right)^{-1}\;\colon\;f\in\Cscr_{1},\tau_{f}^{A}\in\Sigma_{A}\right\}.

Similarly, if
\alpha,\beta\colon F\Rightarrow E
are pseudonatural transformations between
F
and
E
and if
\rho\colon\alpha\Rrightarrow\beta
is a modification from
\alpha
to
\beta
then there is a natural transformation
\underline{\rho}
providing a
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PC}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PC}(E)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\rho}}

where
\underline{\rho}_{A}\colon\underline{\alpha}A\to\underline{\beta}A
is defined by

\underline{\rho}_{A}:=\left\{\left(\!\,{}_{X}{\rho}\right)_{\!\,{}_{X}{A}}\colon\!\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\!\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\to\!\,_{X}{\beta}\left(\!\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0},\!\,_{X}{A}\in A\right\}.

Sketch.

Here we import parts of the proof of
[
25
, Theorem 4.1.1]
in order to indicate the use of inverses in giving this description. We only focus on the fact that the claimed form of the transition isomorphisms ultimately comes down to the chain of equalities

Because the vertical composition

\underline{\alpha}A=\alpha\circ(A,\Sigma_{A})=(\underline{\alpha}(A),\Sigma_{\underline{\alpha}A})

has object components

\underline{\alpha}(A)=\left\{\!\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\!\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\;\colon\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

we only need to determine the transition isomorphisms
\Sigma_{\underline{\alpha}A}
. From our convention regarding the direction of transition isomorphisms and the definition of the vertical composite
\alpha\circ(A,\Sigma_{A})
we get that

\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}=\left(\!\,{}_{f}{(\alpha\circ(A,T_{A}))}\right)^{-1}=\!\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\!\,{}_{f}{(A,T_{A})}\right)^{-1}\circ\!\,_{f}{\alpha}^{-1}=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha}\right)^{-1}.

The check that this indeed gives a pseudonatural transformation (and that we did not make an error in defining the transition isomorphisms) follows from the fact that the pseudonaturality of
\alpha
implies that if we have any composable pair of morphisms
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z

E(f)\left(\!\,{}_{Y}{\alpha}\left(\tau_{g}^{A}\right)\right)=\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha}\right)_{\!\,{}_{Y}{A}}\circ\!\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\right)\circ\left(\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha}\right)_{F(g)(\!\,_{Z}{A})}\right)^{-1}.

This allows us to compute that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}\circ E(f)\tau_{g}^{\underline{\alpha}A}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\big(\negthinspace\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\big)^{-1}\circ E(f)\big(\negthinspace\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\tau_{g}^{A})\circ\left(\negthinspace\,{}_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\negthinspace\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(f)\big(\negthinspace\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\tau_{g}^{A})\big)\circ E(f)\big(\negthinspace\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

(1)

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\negthinspace\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\negthinspace\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)\circ\negthinspace\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\big)\circ\left(\negthinspace\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\!\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(f)\big(\negthinspace\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

(2)

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\negthinspace\,_{X}{\alpha}(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A})\circ\left(\negthinspace\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\!\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(f)\big(\negthinspace\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\big)\circ\left(\negthinspace\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\!\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(f)\big(\negthinspace\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A}\circ\negthinspace\,_{F}{\phi_{f,g}})\circ\left(\negthinspace\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\!\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(f)\big(\negthinspace\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A})\circ\negthinspace\,_{X}{\alpha}(\negthinspace\,_{F}{\phi_{f,g}})\circ\left(\negthinspace\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\!\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(f)\big(\negthinspace\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}.

We again use the pseudonaturality of
\alpha
to obtain the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\big(E(g)\big(\!\,_{Z}{\alpha}(\!\,_{Z}{A})\big)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\big(\!\,_{Y}{\alpha}(F(g)(\!\,_{Z}{A}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)(F(g)(\!\,_{Z}{A}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(g\circ f)\big(\!\,_{Z}{\alpha}(\!\,_{Z}{A})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(g\circ X)(\!\,_{Z}{A})\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\big(\!\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{E}{\phi_{f,g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\!\,_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\,_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\!\,_{X}{\phi_{f,g}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{g\circ f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}^{-1}}

and substitute the induced equation to get

\!\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A})\circ\!\,_{X}{\alpha}(\!\,_{F}{\phi_{f,g}})\circ\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\!\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(X)\big(\!\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\!\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A})\circ\big(\!\,_{g\circ f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}\circ\!\,_{E}{\phi_{f,g}}=\tau_{g\circ f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}\circ\!\,_{E}{\phi_{f,g}}.

This shows that
\tau_{g\circ f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}\circ\,_{E}{\phi_{f,g}}=\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}\circ E(f)(\tau_{g}^{\underline{\alpha}A})
and hence that
\underline{\alpha}(A)
has the form claimed in the statement of the theorem.

The statement regarding the description of morphisms follows from untangling the combinatorial description of the whiskering of the
2
-morphism
P
by the
1
-morphism
\alpha
in
\mathsf{Bicat}(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
. Explicitly we find

\underline{\alpha}(P)=\alpha\circ P=\alpha\ast P

and so the description

\alpha\ast P=\underline{\alpha}P=\left\{\negthinspace\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\negthinspace\,{}_{X}{\rho}\right)\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

follows. That this is indeed a modification (and hence a morphism in
\mathbf{PC}(E)
) is a straightforward check using the identity

E(f)\left(\!\,{}_{Y}{\alpha}\left(\!\,{}_{Y}{\rho}\right)\right)=\!\,_{f}{\alpha}_{\!\,{}_{Y}{B}}\circ\!\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(F(f)\!\,_{Y}{\rho}\right)\circ\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha}_{\!\,{}_{Y}{A}}\right)^{-1}

which allows us to deduce

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}B}\circ E(f)\big(\!\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\!\,_{Y}{\rho})\big)

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}B}\circ\!\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}\circ\!\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)\!\,_{Y}{\rho}\big)\circ\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\!\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{B})\circ\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}\circ\!\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}\circ\!\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)\!\,_{Y}{\rho}\big)\circ\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\!\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{B})\circ\!\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)\!\,_{Y}{\rho}\big)\circ\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\!\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\!\,_{Y}{\rho}\big)\circ\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\!\,_{X}{\alpha}(\!\,_{X}{\rho}\circ\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\!\,_{X}{\alpha}(\!\,_{X}{\rho})\circ\!\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\!\,_{X}{\alpha}(\!\,_{X}{\rho})\circ\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}

which is what was to be shown.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

It is worth noting that the exact use of the invertibility of the witness transformations of the pseudonatural transformations appears in Lines (
1
) and (
2
) of the proof above. In particular, we actually cancelled a natural isomorphism with its inverse. This shows that if we tried to define the pseudolimit’s 2-dimensional universal property with respect to lax or oplax transformations, this would not work. For similar reasons, we cannot try to define a pseudolimit for a lax or oplax functor in this way. There is a similar result for oplax limits of lax functors (in terms of the category of oplax cones). However, the explicit formula for the transition isomorphisms
\Sigma_{\underline{\alpha}A}
as given in Theorem
2.6
in this oplax cone extension of the result will not be the same.

2.3.
Limits in
\mathbf{PC}(F)

In Section
3
, we will need to discuss (
1
-categorical) limits in pseudocone categories
\mathbf{PC}(F)
as we will need to consider certain pullbacks when investigating the conditions on
\mathbf{PC}(F)
to be a tangent category. To aid us, we recall a handy theorem from
[
25
]
which gives a description of these (
1
-categorical) limits in terms of data recorded by the pseudofunctor
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
. Effectively, this gives that if we have a diagram
d\colon I\to\mathbf{PC}(F)
for which each induced diagram
I\xrightarrow{d}\mathbf{PC}(F)\xrightarrow{\pi_{X}}F(X)
has a limit, and if the functors
F(f)
preserve these limits, then
\mathbf{PC}(F)
has a limit for the diagram
d
whose object-local components are given by the limits in
F(X)
. Explicit details regarding this construction may be found in
[
25
, Theorem 3.1.4, Page 54]
.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
25
]
, Theorem 3.1.4)
.

Let
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor and assume that we have a diagram of shape
d\colon I\to\mathbf{PC}(F)
and for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
let
d_{X}\colon I\to F(X)
denote the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PC}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\imath}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}

where
\tilde{\imath}\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to\prod_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)
is defined by forgetting the transition isomorphisms of an object
(A,T_{A})
. If each
d_{X}
has a limit in
F(X)
and if for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
the functors
F(f)
preserve the limits
\lim d_{X}
then
\mathbf{PC}(F)
admits a limit of
d
.

In conclusion, in this section we have seen a very concrete representation of the pseudolimit of a diagram in
\fCat
indexed by a pseudofunctor together with an analysis of limits in these pseudolimit categories.

3.
Tangent Structures and Pseudolimits

In this section we begin by recalling the notion of tangent category and the notions of morphisms between them. We will then introduce the notion of a tangent indexing functor and use the remainder of the section to discuss some of it properties. First we show that whenever
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is a tangent indexing functor, its pseudolimit
\mathbf{PC}(F)
has a tangent structure induced by the tangent structures on the categories
FX
for the objects
X\in\Cscr^{\op}
. Then we show that our tangent indexing functors are tangent objects in the hom 2-category
\mathsf{Bicat}(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
, as introduced in
[
16
]
.

3.1.
Tangent Categories

Let us recall what it means for a category to be a tangent category following
[
2
]
. These categories are an abstraction of what it means to have a category and tangent bundle functor, assigning to each object its tangent bundle (which is again an object of the same category), together with all the relations and structure we require/expect of such a bundle object. In what follows below, if
\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow\id_{\Cscr}
is a natural transformation for an endofunctor
F\colon\Cscr\to\Cscr
, we write
F_{2}X
for the pullback

\textstyle{F_{2}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{FX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{X}}
\textstyle{FX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{X}}
\textstyle{X}

if it exists in
\Cscr
.

Definition 3.1

(
[
2
]
, Definition 2.3)
.

A category
\Cscr
has a
tangent structure

\Tbb=(T,p,0,+,\ell,c)
when:

(1)

T\colon\Cscr\to\Cscr
is a functor equipped with a natural transformation
p\colon T\Rightarrow\id_{\Cscr}
such that for every object
X\in\Cscr_{0}
all pullback powers of
p_{X}\colon TX\to X
exist and for all
n\in\N
the functors
T^{n}
preserve these pullback powers.

(2)

There are natural transformations
+\colon T_{2}\Rightarrow T
and
0\colon\id_{\Cscr}\to T
for which each map
p_{X}\colon TX\to X
describes an additive bundle in
\Cscr
; i.e.,
p\colon TX\to X
is an internal commutative monoid in
\Cscr_{/X}
with addition and unit given by
+
and
0
, respectively.

(3)

There is a natural transformation
\ell\colon T\Rightarrow T^{2}
such that for any
X\in\Cscr_{0}
, the squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX\times_{X}TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X\times_{TX}T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tp_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\ell_{X}\circ\pi_{1},\ell_{X}\circ\pi_{2}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+_{TX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{TX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{X}}

all commute; i.e.,
(\ell_{X},0_{X})
is a morphism of bundles in
\Cscr
(cf.
[
2
, Definition 2.2]
).

(4)

There is a natural transformation
c\colon T^{2}\Rightarrow T^{2}
such that for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X\times_{TX}T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X\times_{TX}T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tp_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{TX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast+)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle c_{X}\circ\pi_{1},c_{X}\circ\pi_{2}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(+\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{TX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{TX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X}}

commute, i.e.,
(c_{X},\id_{TX})
describes a bundle morphism in
\Cscr
.

(5)

We have the equations
c^{2}=\id_{\Cscr},c\circ\ell=\ell
, and the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\ast\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\ast T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\ast c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\ast T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\ast T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\ast T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\ast c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\ast T}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\ast T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\ast c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\ast T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\ast\ell}

commute in the functor category
[\Cscr,\Cscr]
where
F\ast\alpha
and
\alpha\ast F
denote the left whiskering of a natural transformation
\alpha
by a functor
F
and the right whiskering of a natural transformation
\alpha
by a functor
F
, respectively.

(6)

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast+)_{X}\circ\langle\ell\circ\pi_{1},0_{TX}\circ\pi_{2}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(p_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}\circ p_{X}\circ p_{TX}}

is an equalizer in
\Cscr
.

Remark 3.2
.

In
[
2
]
we find the following useful observations about the parts of Definition
3.1
:

•

Part 1 names
T
the tangent functor of the tangent structure
\Tbb
on
\Cscr
and
p
.

•

Part 2 describes the object
p_{X}\colon TX\to X
as a tangent bundle in
\Cscr
.

•

Part 3 names
\ell
as the vertical lift.

•

Part 4 names
c
as the canonical flip.

•

Part 5 gives the coherence relations on
\ell
and
c
.

•

Part 6 describes the universality of the vertical lift.

Note also that when we say that
\Cscr
is a tangent category, we really mean that
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
is a category equipped with a specific tangent structure
\Tbb
and have omitted the explicit mention of the tangent structure. In fact, this is an abuse of notation; it is possible for a category to have multiple distinct tangent structures
2
2

2

The category
\mathbf{SMan}
of smooth real manifolds is the standard example: there’s the identity tangent structure
\mathbb{I}
and the usual tangent structure defined by taking the bundle functor
T
to send each manifold to its tangent bundle.
, so when we say
\Cscr
is a tangent category we mean that
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
is a category equipped with a specific (potentially unspecified) tangent structure
\Tbb
.

Equally as important to tangent categories are the morphisms of tangent categories. These come in two flavours: one, which is weaker and another which is strong. We will usually work with strong tangent morphisms, but provide the complete definition.

Definition 3.3

(
[
2
]
, Definition 2.7)
.

Let
(\Cscr,\Tbb)=(\Cscr,T,p,0,+,\ell,c)
and
(\Dscr,\Sbb)=(\Dscr,S,q,0^{\prime},\oplus,\ell^{\prime},c^{\prime})
be tangent categories. A
morphism of tangent categories
is a pair
(F,\alpha)\colon(\Cscr,\Tbb)\to(\Dscr,\Sbb)
where
F\colon\Cscr\to\Dscr
is a functor and
\alpha
is a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\circ F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

called a
lax distributive law
. The
following diagrams of functors and natural transformations are required to commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\circ F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\ast p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\ast F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\circ F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\ast 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0^{\prime}\ast F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ T_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}\circ F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\circ F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\ast\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\ast+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\ast F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\circ F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ T^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{2}\circ F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\ast\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}\ast F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S\ast\alpha)\circ(\alpha\ast T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ T^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{2}\circ F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ T^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{2}\circ F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S\ast\alpha)\circ(\alpha\ast T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\ast c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}\ast F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S\ast\alpha)\circ(\alpha\ast T)}

Additionally, we say that the morphism
(F,\alpha)
is
strong
if the distributive law
\alpha
is a natural isomorphism and if
F
preserves the equalizers and pullbacks of the tangent structure
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. Finally, we say that
(F,\alpha)
is
strict
if
\alpha
is the identity transformation.

Remark 3.4
.

In
[
16
]
, what we have defined as a morphism of tangent categories in Definition
3.3
is called instead a
lax
tangent morphism. With this language, a colax morphism of tangent categories
(\Cscr,\Tbb)\to(\Dscr,\Sbb)
(a colax morphism in
[
16
]
) is a pair
(F,\alpha)
where
F\colon\Cscr\to\Dscr
is a functor and
\alpha
is a colax distributive law; i.e., a natural transformation typing as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\circ F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

which satisfies the corresponding analogues of the diagrams defining a morphism of tangent categories in Definition
3.3
. We will use this terminology in Lemma
3.35
and in the proof of Theorem
3.40
, but sparingly elsewhere in this paper.

We now present the notion of a tangent natural transformation so that we can define the
2
-category of tangent categories, morphisms of tangent categories, and tangent natural transformations.

Definition 3.5

(
[
7
]
)
.

A
tangent natural transformation
between morphisms
\rho\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)\colon(\Cscr,\Tbb)\to(\Dscr,\Sbb)
is a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

for which the equation

(\id_{S}\ast\rho)\circ\alpha=\beta\circ(\rho\ast\id_{T})

holds.

Definition 3.6
.

The
2
-category
\fTan
of tangent categories is defined as follows: the objects are tangent categories, the morphisms are tangent category morphisms, and the
2
-cells are tangent natural transformations with compositions as in
\fCat
. The full sub-
2
-category of
\fTan
generated by the strong (lax directional) morphisms of tangent categories will be denoted by
\fTan_{\operatorname{strong}}
.

Remark 3.7
.

We will use the terms “strong lax” and “strong colax” morphism for strong morphisms
(F,\alpha)
when we want to emphasize the direction of the distributive law natural isomorphism
\alpha
as indicated in Remark
3.4
. By default we will give strong morphisms with the distributive law in the lax direction, but we will have reason to consider a strong colax morphism in Lemma
3.35
.

3.2.
The Tangent Structure on Pseudolimits

We now provide our first new key definition: that of a tangent indexing pseudofunctor.

Definition 3.8
.

We say that a pseudofunctor
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is a
tangent indexing functor
if:

(1)

For each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
,
F(X)
is a tangent category; i.e., each category
F(X)
comes equipped with some specific tangent structure
\!\,{}_{X}{\Tbb}=(\!\,_{X}{T},\,_{X}{p},\,_{X}{0},\,_{X}{+},\,_{X}{\ell},\,_{X}{c})
.

(2)

For each
f\colon X\to Y
in
\Cscr_{1}
, there is a natural isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\circ\!\,_{Y}{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{T}\circ F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{f}{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

such that the pair
(F(f),\!\,_{f}{T})\colon(F(Y),\!\,_{Y}{\Tbb})\to(F(X),\!\,_{X}{\Tbb})
is a strong morphism of tangent categories.

(3)

The natural isomorphisms
\!\,{}_{f}{T}^{-1}
vary pseudonaturally in the sense that the collection of functors
\,{}_{X}{T}
indexed by
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and of natural transformations
\!\,{}_{f}{T}^{-1}
indexed by
f\in\Cscr_{1}
form a pseudonatural transformation
T
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}

Remark 3.9
.

We have defined tangent indexing functors as pseudofunctors taking values in the
2
-category of categories; i.e., as pseudofunctors
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
in place of pseudofunctors taking values in
\fTan
for pedagogical reasons so that we can use the pseudolimts in
\fCat
to discuss those in
\fTan
. However, these pseudofunctors are the pseudofunctors which factor as

F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fTan_{\operatorname{strong}}\xrightarrow{\Forget}\fCat.

Although one could check this directly, we prove this by way of by way of the formal theory of tangent objects developed in
[
16
]
in Theorems
3.40
and

3.41
.

Example 3.10
.

As observed in
[
2
, Page 10]
, every category
\Cscr
has a trivial tangent structure

\Ibb_{\Cscr}=(\id_{\Cscr},\iota_{\id},\iota_{\id},\iota_{\id},\iota_{\id},\iota_{\id})

where
\iota_{\id}
is the identity natural isomorphism on the identity functor
\id_{\Cscr}
. Each functor
F\colon\Cscr\to\Dscr
is a strong tangent functor
(F,\iota_{F})\colon(\Cscr,\Ibb_{\Cscr})\to(\Dscr,\Ibb_{\Dscr})
.
3
3

3

This becomes immediate when you notice that the tangent pullbacks are pullbacks of the identity functor over the identity morphism at an object
X
.
Because of this, any pseudofunctor
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is a tangent indexing functor when each category
F(X)
is equipped with the tangent structure
\Ibb_{F(X)}
and the natural isomorphisms
\,{}_{f}{T}
are defined to be
\!\,{}_{f}{T}:=\iota_{F(f)}
.

Given a tangent indexing functor
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
as in Definition
3.8
we can give its category of cones,
\mathbf{PC}(F)
, the structure of a tangent category; i.e., we will define the tangent functor
T\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to\mathbf{PC}(F)
as well as the bundle, zero, addition, vertical lift, and canonical flip natural transformations by making use of Theorem
2.6
. We will show that the pair
T=(\!\,_{X}{T},\!\,_{f}{T}^{-1})
forms a pseudonatural transformation
T\colon F\Rightarrow F
and then show that the various natural constructions give rise to modifications between the various pullback and compositional powers of
T
(after we prove that pullback powers of
T
exist, of course).

Lemma 3.11
.

If
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is a tangent indexing functor then there is a pseudonatural transformation
T\colon F\Rightarrow F
whose object-local components are given by the tangent functors in the tangent structures
(F(X),\,_{X}{\Tbb})
. In particular,
T\colon F\Rightarrow F
determines an endofunctor

\underline{T}\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to\mathbf{PC}(F).

Proof.

The first part of the statement of the lemma is axiomatized, and hence holds by Condition (3) of Definition
3.8
. The second statement follows from Theorem
2.6
.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

In Definition
3.8
, when we ask that the data
(\!\,_{X}{T},\!\,_{f}{T}^{-1})
constitute a pseudonatural transformation
T\colon F\Rightarrow F
, in light of Lemma
3.11
we are asking that the transition isomorphisms on an object
\underline{T}A
take the form

\Sigma_{\underline{T}A}=\left\{\!\,{}_{X}{T}\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)\circ\!\,_{f}{T}_{\!\,{}_{Y}{A}}\;:\;f\in\Cscr_{1},\tau_{f}^{A}\in\Sigma_{A}\right\}.

In particular, this means that the transition isomorphisms are given as a composition of the tangent functor as applied to the transition isomorphisms an object starts with, pre-composed with the tangent morphism’s naturality witness.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a tangent indexing functor. There is a modification
p\colon T\Rrightarrow\iota_{F}
as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F}}
\underset{p}{\Rrightarrow}

where the natural transformations
p_{X}
of the modification are all given by

p_{X}:=\!\,_{X}{p}\colon\!\,_{X}{T}\to\id_{F(X)}

where
\!\,{}_{X}{p}
is the bundle transformation in the tangent structure
(F(X),\!\,_{X}{\Tbb})
.

Proof.

To prove that
p
is a modification it suffices to verify that the diagram of functors and natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\circ\!\,_{Y}{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\circ\id_{F(Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{T}\circ F(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{F(X)}\circ F(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F(f)}\ast\,_{Y}{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{p}\ast\iota_{F(f)}}

commutes for any
f\colon X\to Y
in
\Cscr_{1}
. However, this is equivalent to asking that the diagram of functors and natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\circ\!\,_{Y}{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{T}\circ F(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\ast\!\,_{Y}{\!p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\!p}\ast F(f)}

commutes, which follows from the fact that
(F(f),\!\,_{f}{T})
is a (strong) tangent morphism. Thus
p\colon T\Rrightarrow\iota_{F}
is a modification.
∎

Corollary 3.14
.

If
F
is a tangent indexing functor then there is a natural transformation

\underline{p}\colon\underline{T}\to\id_{\mathbf{PC}(F)}\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to\mathbf{PC}(F).

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a tangent indexing functor. Then there is a modification
0\colon\iota_{F}\Rrightarrow T
as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\underset{0}{\Rrightarrow}

whose object-local natural transformations are given by

\!\,{}_{X}{0}:\id_{F(X)}\Rightarrow\!\,_{X}{T}

where
\!\,{}_{X}{0}
is the zero of the tangent structure
(F(X),\!\,_{X}{\Tbb})
.

Proof.

To prove that
0
is a modification it suffices to verify that the diagram of functors and natural transformations

\textstyle{F(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(f)\circ\id_{F(Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\ast\,_{Y}{0}}
\textstyle{F(f)\circ\!\,_{Y}{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\,{}_{f}{T}}
\textstyle{F(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\id_{F(X)}\circ F(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\,{}_{X}{0}\ast F(f)}
\textstyle{\!\,{}_{X}{T}\circ F(f)}

commutes for any
f\colon X\to Y
in
\Cscr_{1}
. However, this is equivalent to asking that the diagram of functors and natural transformations

\textstyle{F(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\,{}_{X}{0}\ast F(f)}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\ast\!\,_{Y}{0}}
\textstyle{F(f)\circ\!\,_{Y}{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{f}{T}}
\textstyle{\!\,{}_{X}{T}\circ F(f)}

commutes, which follows from
(F(f),\!\,_{f}{T})
being a strong tangent morphism.
∎

Corollary 3.16
.

If
F
is a tangent indexing functor then there is a natural transformation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PC}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PC}(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\mathbf{PC}(F)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{0}}

Recall that it is a centrally important aspect of tangent categories that we have a notion of additive tangent bundles. As such, we will require a short discussion of finite pullback powers of the tangent bundle
\underline{p}\colon\underline{T}\Rightarrow\id_{\mathbf{PC}(F)}
in
\mathbf{PC}(F)
. Perhaps unsurprisingly, this will make use of Theorem
2.8
above.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a tangent indexing functor and let
A\in\mathbf{PC}(F)_{0}
. Then for any
n\in\N
the pullback
T_{n}A
exists in
\mathbf{PC}(F)
.

Proof.

Since
T_{0}A=A
and
T_{1}A=p_{A}\colon TA\to A
, it suffices to show that
T_{2}A
exists. For this consider the cospan

TA\xrightarrow{\underline{p}_{A}}A\xleftarrow{\underline{p}_{A}}TA

in
\mathbf{PC}(F)
. At each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
this gives the cospan

\!\,{}_{X}{T}\left(\!\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\xrightarrow{\,{}_{X}{\!p}_{\!\,{}_{X}{A}}}\!\,_{X}{A}\xleftarrow{\!\,{}_{X}{p}_{\!\,{}_{X}{A}}}\!\,_{X}{T}\left(\!\,{}_{X}{A}\right)

in the tangent category
(F(X),\!\,_{X}{\Tbb})
. Because
F(X)
is a tangent category with tangent structure
\!\,{}_{X}{\Tbb}
, we have that
\!\,{}_{X}{T}_{2}\left(\!\,{}_{X}{A}\right)
exists in
F(X)
for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
. Furthermore, because each functor
F(f)
is the functor component of a strong tangent morphism
(F(f),\!\,_{f}{T})
, each functor
F(f)
preserves each tangent pullback. It thus follows from Theorem
2.8
that the pullback

\textstyle{T_{2}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{p}_{A}}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{p}_{A}}
\textstyle{A}

exists.
∎

We now prove that the pullback powers of the tangent functor
\underline{T}_{n}
on
\mathbf{PC}(F)
arise themselves as pseudocone functors coming from pseudonatural transformations. While not strictly speaking necessary for the existence of the pullbacks
T_{2}
, this shows how the natural transformations
\!\,{}_{f}{T_{2}}
connecting
F(f)\circ\!\,_{Y}{T_{2}}\Rightarrow\!\,_{X}{T_{2}}\circ F(f)
for any
f\colon X\to Y
arise and are constructed. In particular, it shows that we can use Theorem
2.6
to define our addition natural transformation.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a tangent indexing functor. There is a pseudonatural transformation
T_{2}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}}

whose functors
\!\,{}_{X}{T_{2}}
are given by sending an object
A\in F(X)_{0}
to the pullback
\!\,{}_{X}{T_{2}}(A)
of the cospan
\!\,{}_{X}{T}(A)\xrightarrow{\,{}_{X}{p}_{A}}A\xleftarrow{\,{}_{X}{p}_{A}}\!\,_{X}{T}(A)
.

Proof.

We need to construct the natural isomorphism
\!\,{}_{f}{T_{2}}\colon F(f)\circ\!\,_{Y}{T_{2}}\xRightarrow{\cong}\!\,_{X}{T_{2}}\circ F(f)
. For this let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism in
\Cscr_{1}
and let
A\in F(Y)_{0}
. Consider the cube:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\left(\!\,{}_{Y}{T_{2}}(A)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\left(\!\,{}_{Y}{T}(A)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{T_{2}}\left(F(f)(A)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{T}\left(F(f)(A)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\left(\!\,{}_{Y}{T}(A)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{T}\left(F(f)(A)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\!\,_{Y}{\pi_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\!\,_{Y}{\pi_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{T}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F(f)\ast\!\,_{Y}{p})_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\!\,_{X}{\!p}\ast F(f))_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F(f)\ast\!\,_{Y}{p})_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{T}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\!\,_{X}{\!p}\ast F(f))_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\pi_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\!\pi_{1}}}

We define
(\!\,_{f}{T_{2}})_{A}
to be the unique dotted arrow which renders the cube above commutative and define
\,{}_{f}{T_{2}}:=\{(\!\,_{f}{T_{2}})_{A}\;|\;A\in F(Y)_{0}\}
. That
\!\,{}_{f}{T_{2}}
is a natural transformation is routinely checked by using that if
\rho\in F(X)(A,B)
then the morphism
\!\,{}_{X}{T_{2}}(\rho)
is the unique map making the cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!T_{2}}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!T}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!T_{2}}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!T}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!T}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!T}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!\pi_{2}^{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!\pi_{1}^{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{T}(\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{p}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!p}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!p}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!T}(\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!p}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!\pi_{2}^{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{X}{\!\pi_{1}^{B}}}

commute.
We also note that
\!\,{}_{f}{T_{2}}
is a natural isomorphism because the functor
F(f)
, as a strong tangent morphism, preserves tangent pullbacks and the maps which constitute
\!\,{}_{f}{T_{2}}
witness this.
Finally the pseudonaturality of the collection
(\!\,_{X}{\!T_{2}},\!\,_{f}{T_{2}})
follows from the fact that the object functors
\!\,{}_{X}{\!T_{2}}
are defined by taking pullbacks of the pseudonatural transformation
T
while the natural isomorphisms
\!\,{}_{f}{T_{2}}
are defined by the limit preservation isomorphisms of
F(f)
.
∎

Corollary 3.19
.

For any
n\in\N
there is a pseudonatural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n}}

whose underlying fibre functors
\,{}_{X}{T_{n}}
are the
n
-fold tangent pullback powers.

Proof.

This is a routine induction with
T_{0}A:=A
,
T_{1}A:=TA
, and taking
T_{2}A
as the base case for an induction involving finite pullback powers.
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

If
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is a tangent indexing functor then the
n
-fold tangent pullback functor
\underline{T_{n}}\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
arises as the functor associated to the pseudonatural transformation
T_{n}:F\Rightarrow F
. In particular,
[\underline{T}]_{2}=\underline{T_{2}}
.

Proof.

The existence of
\underline{T_{n}}
for all
n\in\N
follows from applying Theorem
2.6
to Corollary
3.19
. Finally, note that if
(A,T_{A})
is an object in
\mathbf{PC}(F)
then
\underline{T_{2}}A=\underline{T}_{2}A
. Thus the fact that
[\underline{T}]_{2}=\underline{T_{2}}
comes down to noticing that for any
f\colon X\to Y
in
\Cscr_{1}
, the naturality isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\circ\,_{Y}{T_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{T_{2}}\circ F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{T_{2}}}

of the pseudonatural transformation
T_{2}
are exactly the limit preservation isomorphisms induced by
F(f)
preserving tangent pullbacks. As such the transition isomorphisms of
T_{\underline{T_{2}}A}
coincide with the transition isomorphisms of
T_{\underline{T}_{2}A}
and so
\underline{T}_{2}=\underline{T_{2}}
.
∎

Corollary 3.21
.

Let
n,m\in\N
and let
F
be a tangent indexing functor on
X
. Then
\underline{T}^{n}\circ\underline{T}_{m}\cong\underline{T}_{m}\circ\underline{T}^{n},
i.e., powers of
\underline{T}
preserve all tangent pullbacks
\underline{T}_{m}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the fact that object-locally we have natural isomorphisms

\!\,{}_{X}{\!T^{n}}\circ\!\,_{X}{\!T_{m}}\cong\!\,_{X}{\!T_{m}}\circ\!\,_{X}{\!T^{n}}

by virtue of
(F(X),\!\,_{X}{\Tbb})
being a tangent category and each
\!\,{}_{X}{\!T}
being a tangent functor.
∎

We now use the tangent pullback functor
\underline{T_{2}}=\underline{T}_{2}
we just built in order to prove that we can add pseudolimit bundles by making sure that the object-local pieces of the bundle addition on
\underline{T}_{2}A
are given by the bundle addition
\,{}_{X}{+}\colon\!\,_{X}{T_{2}}(\!\,_{X}{A})\to\!\,_{X}{T}(\!\,_{X}{A})
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
. Then there is an addition modification
+\colon T_{2}\Rrightarrow T
as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\underset{+}{\Rrightarrow}

whose natural transformations are given by

\,{}_{X}{+}\colon\!\,_{X}{\!T_{2}}\to\!\,_{X}{\!T}

where
\,{}_{X}{+}
is the bundle addition transformation in the tangent structure
(F(X),\!\,_{X}{\Tbb})
.

Proof.

To prove that
+
is a modification it suffices to prove that the diagram of functors and natural transformations

\textstyle{F(f)\circ\!\,_{Y}{\!T_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\,{}_{f}{T_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\ast\,_{Y}{\!+}}
\textstyle{F(f)\circ\!\,_{Y}{\!T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\,{}_{f}{T}}
\textstyle{\!\,{}_{X}{\!T_{2}}\circ F(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{+}\ast F(f)}
\textstyle{\!\,{}_{X}{\!T}\circ F(f)}

commutes. However, this follows immediately because
(F(f),\!\,_{f}{T})
is a strong tangent morphism.
∎

Corollary 3.23
.

For any tangent indexing functor
F
on
X
there is an addition natural transformation
\underline{+}\colon\underline{T}_{2}\Rightarrow\underline{T}
.

Proof.

Apply Theorem
2.6
to the modification
+\colon T_{2}\Rrightarrow T
.
∎

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
F
be a tangent indexing functor on
X
. For any
A\in\mathbf{PC}(F)_{0}
, the tangent bundle
\underline{T}A
is an additive bundle in
\mathbf{PC}(F)
, i.e.,

\left(\underline{p}_{A}\colon\underline{T}A\to A,\underline{+}_{A}\colon\underline{T}_{2}A\to\underline{T}A,\underline{0}_{A}\colon A\to\underline{T}A\right)

is a commutative monoid in
\mathbf{PC}(F)_{/A}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\underline{0}_{A}
is a left-and-right unit for
\underline{p}_{A}\colon\underline{T}A\to A
and that bundle addition
\underline{+}_{A}
is commutative and associative over
A
. However, checking that the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}A\times_{A}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}_{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{A}\underline{T}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}_{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\underline{T}A}\times\underline{0}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{+}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{0}_{A}\times\id_{\underline{T}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{+}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}_{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}_{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{+}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{+}_{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{T}A\times_{A}\underline{T}A)\times\underline{T}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}_{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}A\times_{A}(\underline{T}A\times_{A}\underline{T}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{T}_{2}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{+}_{A}\times\id_{\underline{T}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{+}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\underline{T}A}\times\underline{+}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{+}_{A}}

all hold, where
s
is the product-swapping map, may be done object-locally, i.e., it may be checked over each category
F(X)
. However, because each category
(F(X),\,_{X}{\Tbb})
is a tangent category we have that
\,{}_{X}{T}(\,_{X}{A})
is an additive bundle over
\,{}_{X}{A}
and so each desired object-local diagram commutes. Thus
\underline{T}A
is an additive
A
-bundle.
∎

We now recall a basic construction in
2
-category and bicategory theory in order to better understand how to define and work with the composite
\underline{T}^{2}
directly from its descent incarnation from the vertical composition
T^{2}=T\circ T
of pseudonatural transformations. If
F,G,H\colon\Cfrak\to\Dfrak
are two pseudofunctors between
2
-categories and if we have pseudonatural transformations as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cfrak}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfrak}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

then the vertical composite
\beta\circ\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow H\colon\Cfrak\to\Dfrak
has object functors defined by, for all
X\in\Cfrak_{0}

(\beta\circ\alpha)_{X}=\beta_{X}\circ\alpha_{X},

while it has morphism natural transformations defined by, for all
f\colon X\to Y\in\Cfrak_{1}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta\circ\alpha)_{f}=(\beta_{Y}\ast\alpha_{f})\circ(\beta_{f}\ast\alpha_{X}).}

From this we get that
\underline{T}^{2}\colon\mathbf{PC}(F)\to\mathbf{PC}(F)
arises as the functor associated to the pseudonatural transformation
T^{2}=T\circ T\colon F\Rightarrow F
described by fibre functors

\,{}_{X}{(T^{2})}:=(\,_{X}{T})^{2}

and fibre transformations

\,{}_{f}{T^{2}}:=(\,_{X}{T}\ast\,_{f}{T})\circ(\,_{f}{T}\ast\,_{Y}{T})

for any
f\colon X\to Y
in
\Cscr_{1}
. We will use this observation below in proving the existence of both the verical lift
\underline{\ell}\colon\underline{T}\Rightarrow\underline{T}^{2}
and the canonical flip
\underline{c}\colon\underline{T}^{2}\Rightarrow\underline{T}^{2}
.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
F
be a tangent indexing functor on
X
. There is then a vertical lift modification
\ell\colon T\Rrightarrow T^{2}
as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}}
\underset{\ell}{\Rrightarrow}

whose object-local natural transformations are given by the vertical lifts

\!\,{}_{X}{\ell}\colon\!\,_{X}{T}\to\!\,_{X}{T^{2}}

of the tangent structure
(F(X),\!\,_{X}{\Tbb})
.

Proof.

To show that
\ell
is a modification it suffices to check that the diagram of functors and natural transformations

\textstyle{F(f)\circ\,_{Y}{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\ast\,_{Y}{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{f}{T}}
\textstyle{F(f)\circ\,_{Y}{T^{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{f}{T^{2}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{T}\circ F(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\ell}\ast F(f)}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{T^{2}}\circ F(f)}

commutes for any
f\colon X\to Y
in
\Cscr_{1}
. However, this is equivalent to asking that the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)\circ\,_{Y}{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{f}{T}}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\ast\,_{Y}{\ell}}
\textstyle{F(f)\circ\,_{Y}{T^{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
