\!\,{}_{f}{T}^{-1}
(cf. Theorem
3.41
),
\mathbf{PC}(F)
is the pseudolimit of
F
in
\fCat
if and only if
\mathbf{PC}(F)
is also the pseudolimit of
\mathbf{PC}(\tilde{F})
in
\fTan
. However, both these facts are immediate by Remark
2.5
and the combination of Theorems
4.9
and
4.10
.
∎

5.
Equivariant Descent and Equivariant Categories on Smooth Manifolds and on Varieties

We now change gears and discuss equivariant algebraic geometry and equivariant differential geometry so that we can apply the theory developed in the above sections and define the categories of equivariant descent schemes and equivariant descent manifolds. We also provide this section as a way of illustrating that the perpsective on equivariant descent for both varieties and for manifolds is fibrewise in nature.

To describe equivariant descent (tangent) categories on varieties and smooth manifolds, we begin by recalling some of the formalism developed in
[
25
]
for this purpose. In essence, if
G
is a smooth algebraic group over a field
K
such an equivariant category on a left
G
-variety
X
(or, in the topological formulation, a left
G
-space
X
for a Lie group
G
and a smooth real manifold
X
) has objects given by collections of equivariant descent data as it varies through a pseudofunctor defined on a class of principal
G
-fibrations. These fibrations serve to resolve the action
\alpha_{X}\colon G\times X\to X
. The flexibility of allowing the equivariant category to be determined by a pseudofunctor is that it allows us to give flexible categorical structures with which to probe and study geometric and equivariant-geometric properties; cf. Example
5.19
below for a large list of some of the categories which appear in algebraic geometry, algebraic topology, differntial geometry, and representation theory.

Assumption 5.1
.

If
K
is a field, all
K
-varieties
X
are assumed to be
quasi-projective
. That is
5
5

5

Here we use some scheme-theoretic facts to simplify the story of quasi-projectivity. Because we are working over a field, i.e., over
\Spec K
we can use the definition of quasi-projectivity defined in
[
12
, Page 103]
. This differs from Grothendieck’s definition in
[
10
, Definition 5.3.1]
, but the fact that
\Spec K
is a Noetherian quasi-compact scheme gives that the definition presented here is equivalent to Grothendieck’s definition. For explicit details, see
[
10
, Proposition 5.3.2]
.
there is a projective scheme
Y
with closed immersion
i\colon Y\to\Pbb_{K}^{n}
and an open immersion
j\colon X\to Y
for which the structure map
X\to\Spec K
factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pbb_{K}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

We set the stage for the notion of equivariant category by first defining the objects which we use to resolve the actions first in the variety-theoretic case and second in the topological case. Let
K
be a field and let
G
be an affine
6
6

6

In what follows, much of the theory we develop relies on the group
G
being an affine algebraic group, i.e., an affine group object in the category of
K
-varieties. If we instead work with non-affine groups, the characterization of smooth free
G
-varieties as
G
-varieties with étale locally trivializable actions
G\times\Gamma\to\Gamma
fails. Instead we have to work with what are called locally isotrivial fibrations in order to have a theory which works properly; cf.
[
25
, Proposition 6.1.4, Page 141]
. Because of the extra detail being beyond the scope and nature of this paper, we have elected to work only with affine
G
. Interested readers can see the discussion around
[
25
, Proposition 6.1.4, Page 141]
for what goes into fixing this issue as well as the additional headaches which arise.
smooth algebraic group over
K
. The category of smooth free
G
-varieties,
\Sf(G)
, is defined as follows:

Definition 5.2

(
[
18
]
)
.

We say that
\Gamma
is a
smooth free
G
-variety
if
\Gamma
is a left
G
-variety (over
K
) with a geometric quotient
7
7

7

In the category
\Var
, a quotient of
G
on
X
is simply a coequalizer of the action and projection maps
\alpha_{X},\pi_{2}\colon G\times X\to X
in the category of varieties. A
geometric
quotient of
G
on
X
is then a quotient with the extra property that the quotient topology of the
G
-action on
X
lines up with the Zariski topology on
G\backslash X
and that if
q\colon X\to G\backslash X
is the quotient map then there is an isomorphism of sheaves
\Ocal_{G\backslash X}\cong q_{\ast}\Ocal_{X}^{G}
of the structure sheaf on
G\backslash X
with the pushforward of
G
-invariants on
\Ocal_{X}
.
(cf.
[
20
, Definition 0.6]
)

\quo_{\Gamma}\colon\Gamma\to G\backslash\Gamma
for which
\quo_{\Gamma}
is a smooth
G
-principal fibration; i.e.,
\quo_{\Gamma}
is étale locally trivializable with fibre
G
.

Definition 5.3
.

The category
\Sf(G)
is defined as follows:

•

Objects: Smooth free
G
-varieties
\Gamma
of pure dimension.

•

Morphisms: Smooth
G
-equivariant maps
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
of constant fibre dimension.

•

Composition and Identities: As in
\Var_{/K}
.

We now describe some of the important technical properties
\Sf(G)
has which justify it as a reasonable category with which to do equivariant descent for varieties. That it is sufficient is at least partially justified in practice by the various equivalences of categories described in
[
23
, Proposition 7.1.24, Theorem 7.1.26, Theorem 7.2.14, Theorem 9.0.38]
.

Proposition 5.4

(
[
25
]
, Proposition 6.1.4, Page 141)
.

Let
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
and let
X
be a (quasi-projective) left
G
-variety. Then the variety
\Gamma\times X
is free with a smooth quotient morphism

\quo_{\Gamma\times X}\colon\Gamma\times X\to G\backslash(\Gamma\times X).

Furthermore, if
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is a morphism in
\Sf(G)
then there exists a unique smooth morphism
\overline{f}
for which the diagram

\textstyle{\Gamma\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\times\id_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{\Gamma\times X}}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{\prime}\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{\Gamma^{\prime}\times X}}
\textstyle{G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\overline{f}}
\textstyle{G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X)}

commutes in
\Var_{/K}
.

Remark 5.5
.

The above proposition is false in general if
X
is not a quasi-projective variety or if
G
is a non-affine algebraic group. There are ways around the non-affine-ness of
G
, but we cannot drop the assumption that our
G
-varieties are quasi-projective.

Remark 5.6
.

It is possible to modify the definition of
\Sf(G)
by instead taking the category
\Cscr
of smooth free
G
-varieties which need not be of pure dimension and taking morphisms to be smooth
G
-equivariant morphisms which need not be of constant fibre dimension. If you do this you will arrive at a category which is not equivalent but is sufficient for doing equivariant homological algebra in the sense that the resulting equivariant derived category is equivalent to the equivariant derived category we define. The main difference is that computing the varieties
G\backslash\Gamma
and
G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)
is significantly simpler when
\Gamma
is of constant dimension and having
f
be of constant fibre dimension also simplifies computations of morphisms. The idea of working with the category
\Sf(G)
explicitly is due to Lusztig (cf.
[
18
, Section 1.9]
), although working with arbitrary pseudofunctors defined on
\Sf(G)
(or categories isomorphisc to
\Sf(G)
, at any rate) was first seen in
[
23
]
.

Definition 5.7
.

For any
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
and any left
G
-variety
X
, we will write the quotient variety of
\Gamma\times X
by
G
as

\!\,{}_{\Gamma}{X}:=G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)

in order to reduce the notational complexity of this work.

Corollary 5.8
.

There is a functor
\quo_{(-)\times X}:\Sf(G)\to\Var_{/K}
given on objects by
\Gamma\times X\mapsto\!\,_{\Gamma}{X}
and on morphisms by
f\times\id_{X}\mapsto\overline{f}
.

What we have just seen in the geometric case can be run mutatis mutandis in the topological case, save that there are many simplifying aspects of the theory. Our discussion of the resolution theory of topological groups acting on topological spaces follows the exposition of
[
1
]
, as their work established the modern formalism used for the equivariant derived category of a topological space
8
8

8

It is a pervasive error in the literature to state that the equivariant derived category of a variety was defined in
[
1
]
; however, this is not correct. Throughout
[
1
]
all groups are topological groups (and more often are topological groups which admit acyclic resolutions which are manifolds) and all spaces are topological spaces. That being said, most modern incarnations of the equivariant derived category of a variety can be traced back, in some way, to the ideas put forth in
[
1
]
.
and established the use of the descent-theoretic perspective to equivariant derived categories in general. For what follows we will recall the notion of free spaces for a topological group before moving on to discussing free resolutions and the analogous category and quotient functor in the topological case.

Definition 5.9

(
[
1
]
, Definition 2.1.1)
.

Let
L
be a topological group. We say that a left
L
-space
F
is
free
if the quotient map
q\colon F\to L\backslash F
is locally trivializable with fibre
L
. That is, there is an open cover
\{U_{i}\to L\backslash F\;|\;i\in I\}
of
F
for which in the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{-1}(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

we have a commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{-1}(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\times U_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}

Remark 5.10
.

When
L
is a Lie group and
L\backslash F
is a manifold, what we have called free
L
-spaces here are sometimes known as principal
L
-bundles in the differential geometry literature; cf.
[
14
, Section 10]
for details.

We now define a category of free
L
-spaces, as well as the notion of what it means for a map of topological spaces to be
n
-acyclic for
n\in\N
. These notions are important for equivariant descent and the equivariant derived category on a topological space, as it is the descent through
n
-acyclic resolutions (as
n
varies through
\N
) which control and define the equivariant derived category.

Definition 5.11
.

Let
L
be a topological group. We define the category
\Free(L)
of free
L
-spaces by:

•

Objects: Free
L
-spaces
P
.

•

Morphisms: As in
\Top
.

•

Composition and Identities: As in
\Top
.

The definition above is well-suited to studying the equivariant descent theory of actions of topological groups on topological spaces by freely resolving the action by free spaces. However, we are primarily interested in the cases where the topological group
L
is a Lie group (that is, a group object in
\SMan
) and when the space
M
on which
L
acts is a smooth manifold as well. In this case we wish to resolve the action of
L
on
M
by free
L
-spaces (that is, by principal
L
-bundles) in order to record how the differential structures of the actions vary. To do this, however, we necessitate knowing that the group
L
admits free
L
-spaces which are themselves, smooth manifolds. Luckily, in most cases of interest to representation theorists, there are positive answers to this question.

Remark 5.12
.

It is known from
[
1
, Lemma 3.1, Page 34]
that if
L
is either a closed subgroup of
\GL_{n}(\R)
for some
n\in\N
or if
L
is a Lie group with finitely many connected components, then
L
has free spaces which are smooth manifolds. In the case
L
is a linear group, the Stiefel manifold
S_{n,k}
of
k
-frames in
\R^{n+k}
is a free
L
-space which is smooth manifold for all
k
.

Because of the remark above, we make an assumption which allows us to ensure that we have manifold free
L
-spaces. This also will allow us to have a tractable handle on the principal bundle theory of the group
L
which stays inside the category
\SMan
.

Assumption 5.13
.

We assume that
L
is a Lie group which is either a closed subgroup of some
\GL_{n}(\R)
or a Lie group with finitely many connected components.

Under these assumptions, we have that
L
admits free
L
-spaces which are smooth manifolds. In this case we will wont to focus on the category of these free
L
-manifolds as opposed to all possible free
L
-spaces so as to stay within
\SMan
.

Definition 5.14
.

Let
L
be a Lie group satisfying Assumption
5.13
. We define the category
\FMan(L)
of free
L
-manifolds as follows:

•

Objects: Free
L
-spaces
M
which are smooth manifolds.

•

Morphisms: Smooth
L
-equivariant maps.

•

Composition and Identities: As in
\Free(L)
.

In the case that
M
is a free
L
-manifold, the group action
L\times M\to M
is free and acts on the fibres of
M
by translation in the
L
-atlas of
M
. This allows us to put a smooth manifold structure on the quotient space
L\backslash M
and also deduce that the morphism
\quo_{M}\colon M\to L\backslash M
is a submersion of smooth manifolds, as the local trivialization of the quotient map
\quo_{M}\colon M\to L\backslash M
gives the quotient map
\quo_{M}
the structure of a differential
L\backslash M
-bundle. We collect the observations we require on free smooth
L
-manifolds in order to use later.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
L
be a Lie group satisfying Assumption
5.13
and let
M
be a smooth left
M
-spaces, i.e., a smooth manifold
M
with a smooth group action
\alpha\colon L\times M\to M
. Then:

(1)

If
F
is a free
L
-manifold, then the quotient morphism
\quo_{F}\colon F\to L\backslash F
is a submersion.

(2)

For any manifold
F
in
\FMan(L)
, the quotient space
L\backslash(F\times M)
is a smooth manifold and the quotient morphism
\quo_{F\times M}\colon F\times M\to L\backslash(F\times M)
is a submersion of manifolds.

(3)

For any morphism
f\colon F\to E
in
\FMan(L)
there is a unique morphism
\overline{f}\colon L\backslash(F\times M)\to L\backslash(E\times M)
fitting into the commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\backslash(F\times M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\backslash(E\times M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{F\times M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{E\times M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}

In particular, there is a functor
\quo_{(-)\times M}\colon\FMan(L)\to\SMan
given on objects by
F\mapsto L\backslash(F\times M)
and on morphisms by
f\mapsto\overline{f}
.

(4)

For any morphism
f\colon F\to E
of free
L
-spaces, the map
\overline{f}\colon L\backslash(F\times M)\to L\backslash(E\times M)
is a local diffeomorphism and hence a submersion.

Proof.

For (1) we note that the discussion of the fact that
L\backslash F
is smooth is given directly prior to the statement of the proposition. That the map
\quo_{F}\colon F\to L\backslash F
is a submersion follows from
[
14
, Lemma 10.3]
and the fact that the local trivialization of the quotient map makes
F
into a differential
L\backslash F
-bundle.

The content of (2) is precisely
[
14
, Theorem 10.7.(1)]
.

To prove (3) note that the manifold
L\backslash(F\times M)
is the coequalizer of
\alpha_{F\times M},\pi_{2}\colon L\times(F\times M)\to F\times M
in
\SMan
by virtue of (2). As such, the existence of the map
\overline{f}
follows immediately from the universal property of the coequalizers. Finally, the functoriality of this construction in
\FMan(L)
is immediate from (1), (2), and the existence of uniqueness of the maps
\overline{f}
.

To prove (4) note that since the space
F
is locally trivial, any
L
-equivariant morphism
F\to E
of free
L
-spaces is necessarily an isomorphism
9
9

9

Since we have a local trivialization of
F
and this trivialization is
L
-equivariant, any equivariant endomorphism over the trivialization must be a map
U_{i}\times L\to U_{i}\times L
given by some
L
-equivariant automorphism of
L
. Gluing these patches show that any such endomorphism is an automorphism and then descent to maps between trivializations force diffeomorphisms between the trivializations which glue to a global diffeomorphism.
, and hence a submersion, in
\FMan(L)
. Consequently, the map

(f\times\id_{M})\colon F\times M\longrightarrow E\times M

is an isomorphism and hence a submersion. Since the morphism
\overline{f}
is the functorial image of the isomorphism
f\times\id_{M}
,
\overline{f}
is an isomorphism of smooth manifolds and hence a local diffeomorphism, i.e., an étale map of smooth manifolds. Since étale maps of smooth manifolds are both immersions and submersions, the result follows.
∎

We now recall the definition of a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor and then the equivariant category on a variety.

Definition 5.16
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group over a field
K
with
X
a left
G
-variety and let
L
be a Lie group satisfying Assumption
5.13
with
M
a smooth left
L
-manifold with smooth
L
-action. A
pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
on
X
is a pair
(F,\overline{F})
of pseudofunctors
F\colon\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and
\overline{F}\colon\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
making the diagram

\textstyle{\Sf(G)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\Var_{/K}^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{F}}
\textstyle{\fCat}

commute. Similarly, a
pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
on
M
is a pair
(E,\overline{E})
of pseudofunctors
E\colon\FMan(L)^{\op}\to\fCat
and
E\colon\SMan^{\op}\to\fCat
making the diagram

\textstyle{\FMan(L)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times M}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{E}
\textstyle{\SMan^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{E}}
\textstyle{\fCat}

commute.

Remark 5.17
.

The term “pre-equivariant” in the definition above seems strange at first glance. However, we call the pair
(F,\overline{F})
a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor for the following (arguably aesthetic) reasons and as a way of organizing one’s thoughts.

In practice, an equivariant category (such as a category of equivariant sheaves, the equivariant derived category, a category of equivariant perverse sheaves, equivariant local systems, equivariant quasi-coherent sheaves, equivariant
\Dcal
-modules, etc.) is determined by three aspects: A pseudofunctor defined on a “sufficiently global” category (such as the category of varieties, smooth varieties, smooth manifolds, etc.), all possible equivariant descent data recorded by the quotient functor
\quo_{(-)\times X}\colon\Sf(G)\to\Var_{/K}
or
\quo_{(-)\times M}\colon\FMan(L)\to\SMan
, and the fact that in forming the equivariant category itself we take what amounts to all possible effective descent data mediating between the group action resolutions and the pseudofunctor itself. In this sense, by giving the pseudofunctors
F
and
\overline{F}
, we furnish ourselves with the first two pieces of equivariant descent data. It’s only after taking global sections (as in the Cartesian fibrations of the elements of
F
) that we arrive with an actual equivariant category, so a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor is “pre-equivariant” in the sense that it is the information with which we can begin to consider equivariant descent, but have not yet imposed any descent conditions and as such have not yet made the information completely equivariant.

Definition 5.18
.

Let
F=(F,\overline{F})\colon\Sf(G)^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
. The
equivariant category
of
F
over
X
is defined to be the category
F_{G}(X):=\mathbf{PC}(F)
.

Example 5.19
.

Here are some examples of equivariant categories which appear in equivariant algebraic geometry, equivariant differential geometry, and equivariant algebraic topology. As before,
G
here is a smooth algebraic group over a field
K
,
X
is a left
G
-variety,
L
is a Lie group satisfying Assumption
5.13
, and
M
is a smooth left
L
-space with
\SMan
group action
L\times M\to M
.

(1)

If
\Dbb,\Dbb^{b}
are the pseudofunctors

\textstyle{\Sf(G)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\Dbb}
\textstyle{\Var_{/K}^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{c}(-)}
\textstyle{\fCat}

\textstyle{\Sf(G)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\Dbb^{b}}
\textstyle{\Var_{/K}^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{c}^{b}(-)}
\textstyle{\fCat}

with

\Dbb(\Gamma):=D_{c}(\XGamma),\quad\Dbb^{b}(\Gamma)=D_{c}^{b}(\XGamma)

on objects
\Gamma
and

\Dbb(f)=L\of^{\ast},\quad\Dbb^{b}(f\times\id_{X})=L\of^{\ast}.

then
\Dbb^{b}_{G}(X)=D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
\Dbb_{G}(X)=D_{G}(X)
are the bounded and unbounded equivariant derived categories, respectively. Note that the category
D_{c}^{b}(\XGamma)
is the derived category of bounded constructible étale sheaves on
\XGamma
; cf.
[
6
, Definition II.4.7]
for details regarding constructible sheaves.

(2)

The differential-geoemtric/algebraic-topological equivariant derived category on
M
is described as follows. Define the pre-equivariant pseudofunctors
\Dbb,\Dbb^{b}
by

\textstyle{\FMan(L)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\Dbb}
\textstyle{\SMan^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{c}(-)}
\textstyle{\fCat}

\textstyle{\FMan(L)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times L}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\Dbb^{b}}
\textstyle{\SMan^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D^{b}_{c}(-)}
\textstyle{\fCat}

where
\Dbb(F):=D_{c}(L\backslash(F\times M))
,
\Dbb^{b}(F):=D_{c}^{b}(L\backslash(F\times M))
, and
\Dbb(f)=\overline{f}^{\ast}
while
\Dbb^{b}(f)=\overline{f}^{\ast}
. Then the equivariant categories
D_{L}(M)=:\Dbb_{L}(M)
and
D_{L}^{b}(M)=:\Dbb^{b}_{L}(M)
are the topological equivariant derived and bounded derived categories on
M
, respectively.

(3)

If
\underline{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({-};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
is the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor

\textstyle{\Sf(G)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({-};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}}
\textstyle{\Var_{/K}^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{c}^{b}(-;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\textstyle{\fCat}

with

\underline{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\Gamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}=\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

on objects and

\underline{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({f};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}=\of^{\ast}

then
\underline{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}_{G}=\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
is the equivariant derived category of
\ell
-adic sheaves on
X
(for
\ell>0
an integer prime with
\gcd(\operatorname{char}(X),\ell)=1
if
\operatorname{char}(X)>0
).

(4)

If
\Sbb
is the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor

\textstyle{\Sf(G)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\Sbb}
\textstyle{\Var_{/K}^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Ab(-;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\textstyle{\fCat}

where

\Sbb(\Gamma):=\Ab(\XGamma;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

on objects and

\Sbb(f):=\of^{\ast}

on morphisms then
\Sbb_{G}(X)=:\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
is the category of equivariant
\ell
-adic sheaves on
X
for
\ell>0
an integer prime with
\gcd(\operatorname{char}(X),\ell)=1
if
\operatorname{char}(X)>0
. Note that
\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
is equivalent to the usual category of equivariant
\ell
-adic sheaves by
[
23
, Proposition 7.1.26]
.

(5)

If
\Pbb
is the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor

\textstyle{\Sf(G)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\Pbb}
\textstyle{\Var_{/K}^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Per(-;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\textstyle{\fCat}

with

\Pbb(\Gamma):=\Per(\XGamma;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

and

\Pbb(f):={}^{p}\of^{\ast}

then
\Pbb_{G}(X)=:\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
is the category of equivariant perverse
\ell
-adic sheaves on
X
. Note that
\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
is equivalent to the usual category of equivariant
\ell
-adic perverse sheaves by
[
23
, Theorem 7.1.28]
.

(6)

Let
\Lbb
be the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor given by

\textstyle{\Sf(G)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\Lbb}
\textstyle{\Var_{/K}^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Shv((-)_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}})_{\operatorname{lcf}}}
\textstyle{\fCat}

where

\Lbb(\Gamma):=\Shv(\XGamma_{\text{{\'{e}}t}})_{\text{lcf}}

on objects and

\Lbb(f)=\of^{\ast}

on morphisms for
\Shv(\XGamma_{\text{\'{e}}t})_{\text{lcf}}
the category of locally constant finite étale sheaves on
\XGamma
. Then
\Lbb_{G}(X)=:\Shv_{G}(X_{\text{{\'{e}}t}})_{\text{lcf}}
is the category of equivariant locally constant finite étale sheaves on
X
.

(7)

Let
\Q(-):\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
be the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor

\textstyle{\Sf(G)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\Q}
\textstyle{\Var_{/K}^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\QCoh(-)}
\textstyle{\fCat}

\Q(\Gamma):=\QCoh(\XGamma)

on objects and

\Q(f):=\of^{\ast}.

Then
\Q_{G}(X)=:\QCoh_{G}(X)
is the category of equivariant quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
.

(8)

Recall that if
K
is a complete valued field, a
K
-analytic space is a locally ringed space
X=(\lvert X\rvert,\Ocal_{X})
for which there is an open cover
\Ufrak=\{U_{i}\;|\;i\in I\}
of
X
by locally ringed spaces
U_{i}=(\lvert U_{i}\rvert,\Ocal_{U_{i}})
(so in particular there is a gluing

X\cong\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}

in the category
\LocRingSpac
of locally ringed spaces) where for each
i\in I
there is an isomorphism of locally ringed spaces
U_{i}\cong Z_{i}
where
Z_{i}
is an analytic
K
-variety. More explicitly, there is an open subset
V_{i}\subseteq K^{n_{i}}
for some
n_{i}\in\Nbb
and a collection of analytic functions
f_{1},\cdots,f_{m_{i}}
on
V_{i}
for which
\lvert Z_{i}\rvert=\{x\in V_{i}\;|\;f_{1}(x)=\cdots=f_{m_{i}}(x)=0\}
equipped with the subspace topology and
\Ocal_{Z_{i}}\cong\Ocal_{V_{i}}/\Iscr(f_{1},\cdots,f_{m_{i}})
where
\Ocal_{V_{i}}
is the sheaf of analytic functions on
V_{i}
and
\Iscr(f_{1},\cdots,f_{m_{i}})
is the ideal sheaf of
\Ocal_{Z_{i}}
generated by the
f_{j}
. It is well-known that to each finite-type
K
-scheme
X
, there is an associated
K
-analytic space
X_{\text{an}}
which is functorial in
\Sch_{/K}^{\text{f.t.}}
; i.e., there is a functor

(-)_{\text{an}}\colon\Sch^{\text{f.t.}}_{/K}\to\mathbf{AnSp}_{K}

where
\mathbf{AnSp}
is the category of
K
-analytic spaces. We now construct a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
\operatorname{AnCoh}\colon\Sf(G)^{\op}\to\fCat
as follows. Let
\Coh\colon\mathbf{AnSp}_{K}^{\op}\to\fCat
be the coherent module pseudofunctor (so for each
K
-analytic space
Y
,
\Coh(Y)
is the category of coherent sheaves over
Y
and for each morphism
f\colon Y\to Z
of analytic spaces
\Coh(f)=f^{\ast}\colon\Coh(Z)\to\Coh(Y)
is the pullback functor of coherent sheaves. Now define
\operatorname{AnCoh}
by:

\textstyle{\Sf(G)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{AnCoh}}
\textstyle{\Var_{/K}^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Coh\circ(-)_{\operatorname{an}}}
\textstyle{\fCat}

Then
\operatorname{AnCoh}_{G}(X)
describes the category of analytic coherent sheaves on
X
(when regarded as an analytic space).

(9)

Define the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
\Ccal
by

\textstyle{\FMan(L)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{(-)\times M}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\Ccal}
\textstyle{\SMan^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\infty}\Shv(-)}
\textstyle{\fCat}

where

\Ccal(F):=C^{\infty}\Shv(L\backslash(F\times M))

is the category of sheaves of
C^{\infty}
-modules on
L\backslash(F\times M)
and where

\Ccal(f):=f^{\ast}.

Then
\Ccal_{L}(M)=:C^{\infty}\Shv_{L}(M)
describes the category of
L
-equivariant
C^{\infty}
-modules on
M
.

(10)

Let
\Vbb

\textstyle{\FMan(L)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

That

.
and where

.

Remark 5.20
.

Each of the categories
D_{G}(X),

\mathnormal{D}^{\mathnormal{b}}_{{G}}({X})
,
D_{L}(M),

D_{L}(M)^{b}
,
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
,
\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
,
\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}),

\QCoh_{G}(X),

\VVec_{L}(M),

C^{\infty}\Shv_{L}(M)
are additive categories. The categories
D_{G}(X),

D_{L}^{b}(M)
,
D_{L}(M)
,
\mathnormal{D}^{\mathnormal{b}}_{{G}}({X}),
and
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
are all triangulated categories with standard and perverse
t
-structures whose hearts are equivalent to the usual categories of equivariant (
\ell
-adic) sheaves and perverse sheaves; cf.
[
25
, Example 5.1.23, Corollaries 5.1.27, 5.1.28]
. The categories
\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}),\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
, and
\QCoh_{G}(X)
are all Abelian categories; cf.
[
25
, Corollary 3.1.13]
. In general, to show an equivariant category
F_{G}(X)
is enriched in models of a Lawvere theory, Abelian, additive, triangulated, etc., it suffices to show that each category
F(\Gamma)
has the desired structure and that the fibre functors
F(f)
all preserve this structure up to isomorphism; cf., for instance,
[
25
, Lemma 2.4.5, Proposition 3.1.12, Theorem 3.2.3, Proposition 3.2.7, Proposition 3.3.6, Theorem 5.1.10, Theorem 5.1.21]
— these various results show that the categories
F_{G}(X)
satisfy the flavour of structural theory discussed.

6.
The Descent Equivariant Zariski Tangent Structure

In a recent paper,
[
4
]
have proved that the category
\Sch_{/S}
admits a tangent structure for any base scheme
S
. The tangent functor
T\colon\Sch_{/S}\to\Sch_{/S}
sends an
S
-scheme
X
to the (Zariski) tangent fibre of
X
relative to
S
constructed by Grothendieck in
[
11
, Section 16.5]
. In particular, for an
S
-scheme
X
we have
12
12

12

The version of the tangent functor and maps constructed in
[
4
]
is given for affine schemes, but because the
\Sym
functor commutes with tensors on
\QCoh(X)
and because the sheaf of differentials
\Omega_{X/S}^{1}
is a quasi-coherent sheaf, everything regarding this functor may be checked Zariski-locally, i.e., affine-locally on both the target and the base. We’ll describe this more in detail later, but it is worth noting now.

T(X):=T_{X/S}=\Spec\left(\Sym(\Omega^{1}_{X/S})\right).

For an affine scheme
S=\Spec A
and an affine
S
-scheme
X=\Spec B
, the scheme
T_{X/S}=T_{B/A}=\Spec(\Sym(\Omega^{1}_{B/A}))
is an affine scheme. The ring

C=\Sym_{B}\left(\Omega_{B/A}^{1}\right)

is generated by symbols
b,\mathrm{d}b
for
b\in B
generated by the rules that addition and multiplication for symbols from
b\in B
are as in
B
and the Leibniz rule

\mathrm{d}(bb^{\prime})=b^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}(b)+b\,\mathrm{d}(b^{\prime})

holds with
(\mathrm{d}b)\,(\mathrm{d}b^{\prime})=0
and
\mathrm{d}(a)=0
for
a\in A
. With this definition we find that

T_{2}(X)=(T_{B/A})_{2}=\Spec\left(\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})\otimes_{B}\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})\right)

and that

T^{2}(X)=T_{T_{B/A}/A}=\Sym_{\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{B/A})}\left(\Omega^{1}_{\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})/B}\right).

It is worth noting for what follows that the algebra
C
with
T^{2}_{B/A}=\Spec C
,

C=\Sym_{\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})}\left(\Omega^{1}_{\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})/B}\right),

is generated by symbols
b,\mathrm{d}b,\delta b,
and
(\delta\mathrm{d})(b)
for all
b\in B
. Essentially, there is a new derivational neighborhood
\delta
of
\Sym(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})
which gives us a notion of
2
-jets and a distinct “orthogonal” direction of
1
-jets (the
\delta
-direction — the idea is that
T^{2}
roughly corresponds to the mixed partials
\partial^{2}/\partial x\partial y
and
\partial^{2}/\partial y\partial x
when both partials are regarded as nil-square operators).

Theorem 6.1

(
[
4
]
)
.

For affine schemes
\Spec B\to\Spec A
the tangent structure
(\Sch_{/\Spec A},\mathbb{T})
is generated by the maps and functors on affine schemes:

•

The tangent functor is given by
T_{\Spec B/\Spec A}=T_{B/A}=\Spec\left(\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})\right)
.

•

The bundle map
p_{B}\colon T_{B/A}\to\Spec B
is the spectrum of the ring map

q_{B}\colon B\to\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})

generated by
b\mapsto b
.

•

The zero map
0_{B}\colon T_{B/A}\to\Spec B
is the spectrum of the ring map

\zeta_{B}\colon\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})\to B

given by
b\mapsto b,\mathrm{d}b\mapsto 0
.

•

The bundle addition map
+_{B}\colon(T_{B/A})_{2}\to T_{B/A}
is the spectrum of the map

\operatorname{add}_{B}\colon\Sym(\Omega^{1}_{B/A})\to\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{B/A})\otimes_{B}\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{B/A})

given by
b\mapsto b\otimes 1_{B},\mathrm{d}b\mapsto\mathrm{d}b\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathrm{d}b
.

•

The vertical lift
\ell_{B}\colon T_{B/A}\to T^{2}_{B/A}
is given as the spectrum of the ring map

v_{B}\colon\Sym_{\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{B/A})}\left(\Omega^{1}_{\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})/B}\right)\to\Sym_{B}\left(\Omega^{1}_{B/A}\right)

generated by
b\mapsto b,\mathrm{d}b\mapsto 0,\delta b\mapsto 0
,
\delta\mathrm{d}(b)\mapsto\mathrm{d}b
.

•

The canoncial flip is the map
c_{B}\colon T^{2}_{B/A}\to T^{2}_{B/A}
generated as the spectrum of the ring map

\gamma_{B}\colon\Sym_{\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{B/A})}\left(\Omega^{1}_{\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})/B}\right)\to\Sym_{\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{B/A})}\left(\Omega^{1}_{\Sym_{B}(\Omega_{B/A}^{1})/B}\right)

which interchanges
1
-jets, i.e., the map is generated by
b\mapsto b,\mathrm{d}b\mapsto\delta b,\delta b\mapsto\mathrm{d}b
and
(\delta\mathrm{d})b\mapsto(\delta\mathrm{d})b
.

Remark 6.2
.

In order to reduce notational complexity, we will omit the subscripts on the
\Sym
functors if no confusion is likely to arise from said omission.

Definition 6.3
.

We define the Zariski tangent structure on a scheme
S
to be the tangent structure
\Tbb_{\mathbf{Zar}({S})}=(T_{-/S},p,0,+,\ell,c)
on the category
\Sch_{/S}
described by Theorem
6.1
when
S
is affine and the one induced by quasi-coherent gluing of the affine tangent structures when
S
is nonaffine.

This is, in some sense, the “canonical” tangent structure on
\Sch_{/S}
, as the tangent scheme
T_{X/S}
captures
S
-derivations of
X
in the following sense: If
S=\Spec K
for a field
K
and if
\Spec A
is an affine
K
-scheme, then the
A
-points of the tangent scheme
T_{X/S}(A)
satisfy

T_{X/S}(A)=\Sch_{/K}(\Spec A,T_{X/S})\cong\Sch_{/K}\left(\Spec\left(\frac{A[x]}{(x^{2})}\right),X\right)

so in particular
K
-points of
T_{X/S}
give the
1
-differentials
\Omega_{X/K}^{1}
. Moreover, for any closed point
x\in\lvert X\rvert
we have a canonical isomorphism

T_{X/S}(x)\cong\KAlg\left(\frac{\mfrak_{x}}{\mfrak^{2}_{x}},K\right)

of
T_{X/S}(x)
with the Zariski tangent space of
X
over
K
.

Remark 6.4
.

The tangent functor
T_{(-)/S}:\Sch_{/S}\to\Sch_{/S}
is a representable tangent functor. If
W_{S}
is the object

W_{S}:=S\times_{\Spec\Z}\Spec\frac{\Z[x]}{(x^{2})}=S\times_{\Spec\Z}\Z[\varepsilon]

then there is an adjunction
(-)\times_{S}W_{S}\dashv T_{(-)/S}
13
13

13

When
S=\Spec A
is an affine scheme,
W_{S}\cong\Spec A[\varepsilon]\cong\Spec A[x]/(x^{2})
. By the algebraic geometry pullback yoga, we can compute the pullback
X\times_{S}S[\varepsilon]
affine-locally by the pullbacks
X_{j}\times_{A_{i}}\Spec A_{i}[\varepsilon]
for
A_{i}
an affine open of
S
and
X_{j}
an affine open of
X
and then glue them along the corresponding open immersions (this is precisely the content, and method of proof, of
[
12
, Theorem II.3.3]
). Each of the functors
X_{j}\times_{A_{i}}\Spec A_{i}[\varepsilon]
admits the right adjoints
T_{X_{j}/A_{i}}
which in turn may be glued along the adjoint transposes to not only give
T_{X/S}
but also construct the unit and counit morphisms.
. When
S=\Spec R
is an affine scheme and we have
X=\Spec A
and
Y=\Spec B
affine
S
-schemes, we have isomorphisms

\displaystyle\Sch_{/S}(Y,T_{X/S})

\displaystyle\cong\Sch_{/S}(Y,[W_{S},X])\cong\Sch_{/S}(Y\times_{S}W_{S},X)

\displaystyle\cong\Sch_{/S}(\Spec B\times_{R}\Spec R[\varepsilon],\Spec A)\cong\Sch_{/S}\left(\Spec(B\otimes_{R}R[\varepsilon]),\Spec A\right)

\displaystyle\cong\Sch_{/S}\left(\Spec B[\varepsilon],\Spec A\right)\cong\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}(A,B[\varepsilon])

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Der}_{R}(A,B)\cong\RMod(\Omega^{1}_{A/R},B),

so the tangent scheme functor may be thought of as a way of geometrizing the story of algebraic deriviations.

With this tangent construction we now want to establish, for a smooth algebraic group
G
and a left
G
-variety
X
, that there is an equivariant Zariski tangent structure on
X
, i.e., that the category of descent equivariant schemes on
X
is a tangent category. In light of Theorem
3.33
it suffices to prove that the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sf(G)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sch_{/(-)}}

defined by

F(\Gamma):=\Sch_{/\XGamma}

on objects and via the pullback functors

F(f):=\overline{f}^{\ast}\colon\Sch_{/\XGammap}\to\Sch_{/\XGamma}

on morphisms is a tangent pre-equivariant indexing functor on
X
. In particular, this involves showing four main ingredients:

(1)

The pullback functors
\of^{\ast}
preserve tangents in the sense that for any
Z\in\Sch_{/\XGammap}
, if
Z^{\prime}:=Z\times_{\XGammap}\XGamma
then there is an isomorphism

T_{Z^{\prime}/\XGamma}\cong T_{Z/\XGammap}\times_{\XGammap}\XGamma;

(2)

The pullback functors
\of^{\ast}
preserve any and all tangent pullbacks/limits that arise in
\Sch_{/\XGammap}
.

(3)

For any
f\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}\in\Sf(G)_{0}
there is a natural isomorphism
\!\,{}_{f}{T}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sch_{/\XGammap}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sch_{/\XGamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\of^{\ast}\circ T_{-/\XGammap}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{-/\XGamma}\circ\of^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{f}{T}}

for which the pair
(\of^{\ast},\!\,_{f}{T})
is a strong tangent morphism.

(4)

The
(T_{-/\XGamma},\!\,_{f}{T}^{-1})
constitute a pseudonatural transformation
T\colon F\Rightarrow F
.

We will now endeavour to show that these all hold in turn. However, we will do this at a slightly more general level: we will show that the pullback functors are always strong tangent morphisms for any morphism of schemes, as this significantly makes the theory easier to establish and follow notationally. We begin with the observation that the pullback functors do indeed preserve the tangent functors and any and all tangent limits that arise.

Proposition 6.5
.

For any morphism
f\colon X\to Y
of schemes and for any
Y
-scheme
Z
, there is a canonical natural isomorphism of schemes

T_{f^{\ast}Z/X}=T_{Z\times_{Y}X/X}\cong T_{Z/Y}\times_{Y}X.

Proof.

This is
[
11
, Equation IV.16.5.12.2]
.
∎

In what follows when given a scheme map
f\colon X\to Y
we define the natural isomorphism

\!\,{}_{f}{T}\colon f^{\ast}\circ T_{-/Y}\xRightarrow{\cong}T_{-/X}\circ f^{\ast}

by setting each
\!\,{}_{f}{T}_{Z}
, for
Z\to Y
a
Y
-scheme, to be the isomorphism

(f^{\ast}\circ T_{-/Y})(Z)=T_{Z/Y}\times_{Y}X\xrightarrow[\!\,{}_{f}{T}_{Z}]{\cong}T_{f^{\ast}Z/X}=(T_{-/X}\circ f^{\ast})(Z)

described in Proposition
6.5
.

Corollary 6.6
.

For a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
of affine schemes and any affine
Y
-scheme
Z
, if
X\cong\Spec A
,
Y\cong\Spec C
, and
Z\cong\Spec B
then the isomorphism
\!\,{}_{f}{T_{Z}}
is induced by the ring isomorphism
\theta_{B}

\Sym\left(\Omega^{1}_{A\otimes_{C}B/A}\right)\xrightarrow[\theta_{B}]{\cong}\Sym\left(\Omega^{1}_{B/C}\right)\otimes_{C}A

given on pure tensors by

b\otimes a\mapsto b\otimes a,\mathrm{d}(b\otimes a)\mapsto\mathrm{d}b\otimes a.

Lemma 6.7
.

For any morphism
f\colon X\to Y
of schemes, if
Z
is a limit in
\Sch_{/Y}
then
f^{\ast}Z
is a limit in
\Sch_{/X}
. In particular,
f^{\ast}
preserves all tangent pullbacks and equalizers.

Proof.

This is immediate from the fact that the functor
f^{\ast}Z=Z\times_{Y}X
is a pullback and hence commutes with all limits.
∎

With Proposition
6.5
and Lemma
6.7
, in order to prove that
(f^{\ast},\!\,_{f}{T})
is a strong tangent morphism it suffices to prove that the five commutative diagrams displayed in Definition
3.3
actually commute. We will do this by proving six technical lemmas below — one for being able to check things affine locally and then one for each diagram of Definition
3.3
— before concluding in Proposition
6.14
that
(f^{\ast},\!\,_{f}{T})
is always a strong tangent morphism.

Our first technical lemma establishes that for any morphism of schemes
f\colon X\to Y
and for any
Y
-scheme
Z
, checking any of the commuting diagrams in Definition
3.3
may be done affine locally on the base and target. In particular, it suffices in all situations to establish the tangent morphism diagrams for affine schemes.

Lemma 6.8
.

To establish that
(f^{\ast},\!\,_{f}{T})
is a tangent morphism it suffices to assume that
f\colon X\to Y
is a morphism
f\colon\Spec B\to\Spec A
between affine schemes and establish the diagrams of Definition
3.3
for affine schemes over
\Spec A
.

Proof.

We begin by first noting for any scheme map
f\colon X\to Y
and any scheme
Z
over
Y
, the sheaf of
1
-differentials
\Omega_{Z/Y}^{1}
is a quasi-coherent sheaf on
Z
and is given affine-locally on
Z
as
\Omega_{U/Y}^{1}
for
U
an affine open of
Z
. As such, when working with
\Omega_{Z/Y}^{1}
, provided all constructions in sight are quasi-coherent it suffices to work with sheaves on affine schemes and glue appropriately. The symmetric algebra construction
\Sym(\Omega^{1}_{U/Y})
is a ring over
\Gamma(U)
built out of a sheaf of quasi-coherent
\Ocal_{U}
-algebras; in particular,
\Sym(\Omega_{U/Y}^{1})
arises as a
\Gamma(S(\Omega_{U/Y}^{1}))
where
S(\Omega_{U/Y}^{1})
is the sheaf of symmetric algebras on
\Omega_{U/Y}^{1}
and
\Spec(S(\Omega_{U/Y}^{1}))\to U
is affine over
U
.
14
14

14

Because
U
is affine, this implies that
\Spec(S(\Omega_{U/Y}^{1}))
is an affine scheme. Checking global sections gives
\Gamma(S(\Omega_{U/Y}^{1}))\cong\Sym(\Omega_{U/Y}^{1})
. For details, see
[
10
, Proposition 1.3.1, Corollaire I.3.2, Definition 1.7.8]
.
In particular,
\Sym(\Omega^{1}_{U/Y})
is a quasi-coherent construction. Finally, since
\Sym
commutes with tensor products (cf.
[
10
, Section 1.7.5]
,
[
12
, Exercise II.5.16.e]
) and since pullbacks are built affine-locally in any category of relative schemes
\Sch_{/S}
, calculating the sheaves which represent and construct
f^{\ast}T_{Z/Y}
and
T_{Z/Y}\times_{Y}X
may be done affine locally on
Z^{\prime}=Z\times_{Y}X
. However, since the set

\left\{U_{i}\times_{W_{k}}V_{j}\;|\;U_{i}\to Z,V_{j}\to X,W_{k}\to Y\text{affine open},i\in I,j\in J,k\in\Lambda\right\}

is an affine open cover of
Z\times_{Y}X
and
S(f^{\ast}\Omega_{Z/Y}^{1})
and the sheaves of quasi-coherent
\Ocal_{Z\times_{Y}X}
-algebras
S(\Omega_{(Z\times_{Y}X)/X}^{1})
may be calculated and then glued over this cover as

S\left(f^{\ast}\Omega_{U_{i}/{W_{k}}}^{1}\right)=S\left(f^{-1}\Omega^{1}_{U_{i}/W_{k}}\otimes_{f^{-1}\Ocal_{W_{k}}}\Ocal_{V_{j}}\right),\qquad S\left(\Omega^{1}_{(U_{i}\times_{W_{k}}V_{j})/V_{j}}\right)=S\left(\Omega^{1}_{f^{\ast}U_{i}/W_{k}}\right)

respectively, the lemma follows.
∎

We now check the five necessary technical conditions for
(f^{\ast},\!\,_{f}{T})
to be a tangent morphism. First we will establish that
f^{\ast}
and
\!\,{}_{f}{T}
commute suitably with the bundle morphism
p\colon T\Rightarrow\id
and the zero morphism
0\colon\id\Rightarrow T
.

Lemma 6.9
.

For any morphism of schemes
f\colon X\to Y
and any
Y
-scheme
Z
the diagram

\textstyle{f^{\ast}T_{Z/Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(f^{\ast}\circ p)_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\!\,{}_{f}{T_{Z}}}
\textstyle{T_{f^{\ast}Z/X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(p\ast f^{\ast})_{Z}}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}Z}

commutes.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.8
it suffices to prove this for affine schemes. Write
X=\Spec A,Y=\Spec C,
and
Z=\Spec B
in the cospan of schemes:

\textstyle{\Spec B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Spec B}

To show that the diagram

\textstyle{f^{\ast}T_{B/C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\,{}_{f}{T_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}(p_{B})}
\textstyle{T_{A\otimes_{C}B/A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{f^{\ast}B}}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}\Spec B}

commutes in
\Sch_{/X}
it suffices to prove that the diagram

\textstyle{\Sym\left(\Omega^{1}_{A\otimes_{C}B/A}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{B}}
\textstyle{\Sym\left(\Omega_{B/C}\right)\otimes_{C}A}
\textstyle{B\otimes_{C}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{B}\otimes\id_{A}}
\scriptstyle{q_{A\otimes_{C}B}}

commutes in
A\downarrow\Cring
. To check this we find on one hand for any pure tensor
b\otimes a
,

(q_{B}\otimes\id_{A})(b\otimes a)=b\otimes a

while on the other hand

(\theta_{B}\circ q_{B\otimes_{C}A})(b\otimes a)=\theta_{B}(b\otimes a)=b\otimes a,

so the diagram indeed commutes.
∎

Lemma 6.10
.

For any morphism of schemes
f\colon X\to Y
and any
Y
-scheme
Z
the diagram

\textstyle{T_{f^{\ast}Z/X}}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}T_{Z/Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutes.

To show that the diagram

\textstyle{T_{f^{\ast}Z/X}}

Lemma 6.11
.

For any morphism
f\colon X\to Y
of schemes and for any
Y
-scheme
Z
, the diagram

\textstyle{f^{\ast}(T_{Z/Y})_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\!\,_{f}{T_{2}})_{Z}}

\scriptstyle{\!\,{}_{f}{T}_{Z}}
\textstyle{T_{f^{\ast}Z/Y}}

commutes.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.8
it suffices to prove this for affine schemes. Write
X=\Spec A,Y=\Spec C,
and
Z=\Spec B
in the cospan of schemes:

\textstyle{\Spec C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Spec B}

To prove that the diagram

\textstyle{f^{\ast}(T_{Z/Y})_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\!\,_{f}{T_{2}})_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{(f^{\ast}\ast+)_{Z}}
\textstyle{(T_{f^{\ast}Z/X})_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(+\ast f^{\ast})_{Z}}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}T_{Z/Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\,{}_{f}{T}_{Z}}
\textstyle{T_{f^{\ast}Z/Y}}

commutes it suffices to prove that the diagram

\textstyle{\Sym\left(\Omega^{1}_{B\otimes_{C}A/A}\right)\otimes_{B\otimes_{C}A}\Sym\left(\Omega^{1}_{B\otimes_{C}A/A}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\theta_{2})_{B}}
\textstyle{\left(\Sym\left(\Omega^{1}_{B/C}\right)\otimes_{B}\Sym\left(\Omega_{B/C}^{1}\right)\right)\otimes_{C}A}
\textstyle{\Sym\left(\Omega_{B\otimes_{C}A/A}^{1}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{add}_{B\otimes_{C}A}}
\textstyle{\Sym\left(\Omega_{B/C}^{1}\right)\otimes_{C}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{add}_{B}\otimes\id_{A}}

commutes in
A\downarrow\Cring
. Note that by construction we have that
(\theta_{2})_{B}
acts on a pure tensor
(b\otimes a)\otimes(b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime})
by the assignment

(\theta_{2})_{B}\big((b\otimes a)\otimes(b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime})\big)=b\otimes b^{\prime}\otimes aa^{\prime}

and for a differential
\mathrm{d}(b\otimes a)\otimes\mathrm{d}(b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime})
via

(\theta_{2})_{B}\big(\mathrm{d}(b\otimes a)\otimes\mathrm{d}(b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime})\big)=\mathrm{d}b\otimes\mathrm{d}b^{\prime}\otimes aa^{\prime}.

We begin by calculating the top half of the square above, i.e., we compute that for any pure tensor
b\otimes a
in
B\otimes_{C}A
that

\displaystyle\big((\theta_{2})_{B}\circ\operatorname{add}_{B\otimes_{C}A}\big)(b\otimes a)

\displaystyle=(\theta_{2})_{B}(b\otimes a\otimes 1_{B\otimes_{C}A})=b\otimes 1_{B}\otimes a

while on differentials we have

\displaystyle\big((\theta_{2})_{B}\circ\operatorname{add}_{B\otimes_{C}A}\big)\left(\mathrm{d}(b\otimes a)\right)

\displaystyle=(\theta_{2})_{B}\left(\mathrm{d}(b\otimes a)\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathrm{d}(b\otimes a)\right)=\left(\mathrm{d}b\otimes 1_{B}\right)\otimes a+\left(1_{B}\otimes\mathrm{d}b\right)\otimes a.

Let us now compute the bottom half of the square above. Fix a pure tensor
b\otimes a
and observe that on one hand we have

\big((\operatorname{add}_{B}\otimes\id_{A})\circ\theta_{B}\big)(b\otimes a)=(\operatorname{add}_{B}\otimes\id_{A})(b\otimes a)=b\otimes 1_{B}\otimes a

while on the other hand for differentials we get

\big((\operatorname{add}_{B}\otimes\id_{A})\circ\theta_{B}\big)\left(\mathrm{d}(b\otimes a)\right)=(\operatorname{add}_{B}\otimes\id_{A})(\mathrm{d}b\otimes a)=(\mathrm{d}b\otimes 1_{B})\otimes a+(1_{B}\otimes\mathrm{d}b)\otimes a.

It follows that
(\theta_{2})_{B}\circ\operatorname{add}_{B\otimes_{C}A}=(\operatorname{add}_{B}\otimes\id_{A})\circ\theta_{B}
so the diagram indeed commutes.
∎

15

15

For potentially obvious reasons it is best to avoid the notation

\!\,{}_{f}{T^{2}_{B}}
and is given on generators by

b\otimes a

\displaystyle\big((v_{B}\otimes\id_{A})\circ\sigma_{B}\big)(b\otimes a)

\displaystyle\big(\theta_{B}\circ v_{B\otimes_{C}A}\big)\big(\mathrm{d}(b\otimes a)\big)

\displaystyle=\theta_{B}(0)=0;

\displaystyle\big(\theta_{B}\circ v_{B\otimes_{C}A}\big)\big(\delta(b\otimes a)\big)

\displaystyle=\theta_{B}(0)=0;

\displaystyle\big(\theta_{B}\circ v_{B\otimes_{C}A}\big)\big(\delta\mathrm{d}(b\otimes a)\big)

\displaystyle=\theta_{B}\big(\mathrm{d}(b\otimes a)\big)=\mathrm{d}b\otimes a.

Because all assignments agree on all generators, it follows that the square commutes.
∎

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sch_{/Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sch_{/X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sch_{-/Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sch_{/X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{-/Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g\circ f)^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{-/X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g\circ f)^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{g\circ f}{T}^{-1}}

In particular,
(T_{-/X},\!\,_{f}{T}^{-1})
describe the data of a pseudonatural transformation

T\colon\Sch_{/-}\Rightarrow\Sch_{/-}\colon\Sch\to\fCat.

Proof.

After expanding out the pasting diagrams, we see that we are asking to show that the morphism
\alpha
encoding the first pasting diagram defined via the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{-/X}\circ f^{\ast}\circ g^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\circ T_{-/Y}\circ g^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g\circ f)^{\ast}\circ T_{-/Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\circ g^{\ast}\circ T_{-/Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{f}{T^{-1}}\ast g^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\circ\!\,_{g}{T^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{f,g}\ast T_{-/Z}}

is equal to the morphism
\beta
encoding the second pasting diagram defined via the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{-/X}\circ f^{\ast}\circ g^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{-/X}\circ(g\circ f)^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g\circ f)^{\ast}\circ T_{-/Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{-/X}\ast\phi_{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\,{}_{g\circ f}{T^{-1}}}

To show this we let
W
be an arbitrary
Z
-scheme. From the discussion prior to the statement of the proposition, we know it suffices to prove the equality of
\alpha
and
\beta
on affine schemes. For this we assume that
X\cong\Spec A
,
Y\cong\Spec B
,
Z\cong\Spec C
, and
W\cong\Spec R
in the diagram

\textstyle{\Spec R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Spec B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\Spec C}

of schemes.

We will now calculate how
\alpha
acts on its domain. It is a straightfoward unwiniding of definitions to check that
\alpha_{W}=\alpha_{R}
is the spectrum of the composite of ring maps

\Gamma(\alpha^{\sharp}_{R})=\left(\theta_{R\otimes_{C}B}^{A,B}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\left(\theta_{R}^{B,C}\right)^{-1}\otimes\id_{A}\right)\circ\omega_{\Sym(\Omega^{1}_{R/C})}^{A,B,C}

which is seen visually as

\Sym\left(\Omega_{R/C}^{1}\right)\otimes_{C}A\xrightarrow{}\left(\Sym\left(\Omega_{R/C}^{1}\right)\otimes_{C}B\right)\otimes_{B}A\xrightarrow{}\left(\Sym\left(\Omega_{R\otimes_{C}B/B}\right)\right)\otimes_{B}A\to\Sym\left(\Omega_{(R\otimes_{C}B)\otimes_{B}A/A}\right).

Fix now a pure tensor
r\otimes a
in
\Sym(\Omega_{R/C}^{1})\otimes_{C}A
with
r\in R
and
a\in A
. We then calculate that

\displaystyle\Gamma(\alpha_{R}^{\sharp})(r\otimes a)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\theta_{R\otimes_{C}B}^{A,B}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\left(\theta_{R}^{B,C}\right)^{-1}\otimes\id_{A}\right)\circ\omega_{\Sym(\Omega^{1}_{R/C})}^{A,B,C}\right)(r\otimes a)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\theta_{R\otimes_{C}B}^{A,B}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\left(\theta_{R}^{B,C}\right)^{-1}\otimes\id_{A}\right)\right)\big((r\otimes 1_{B})\otimes a\big)

\displaystyle=(r\otimes 1_{B})\otimes a

while for differential pure tensors
\mathrm{d}r\otimes a
we get that

\displaystyle\Gamma(\alpha_{R}^{\sharp})(\mathrm{d}r\otimes a)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\theta_{R\otimes_{C}B}^{A,B}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\left(\theta_{R}^{B,C}\right)^{-1}\otimes\id_{A}\right)\circ\omega_{\Sym(\Omega^{1}_{R/C})}^{A,B,C}\right)(\mathrm{d}r\otimes a)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\theta_{R\otimes_{C}B}^{A,B}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\left(\theta_{R}^{B,C}\right)^{-1}\otimes\id_{A}\right)\right)\big((\mathrm{d}r\otimes 1_{B})\otimes a\big)=\left(\theta_{R\otimes_{C}B}^{A,B}\right)^{-1}\big(\mathrm{d}(r\otimes 1_{B})\otimes a\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\big((r\otimes 1_{B})\otimes a\big).

Because the pure tensors of the form
r\otimes a,\mathrm{d}r\otimes a
for
r\in R
and
a\in A
linearly generate
\Sym(\Omega_{R/C}^{1})\otimes_{C}A
, this completely determines the action of
\Gamma(\alpha_{R}^{\sharp})
after extending linearly.

We now calculate the action of
\beta_{W}=\beta_{R}
on its domain. By construction,
\beta_{R}
is the composite of the ring maps

\Gamma(\beta_{R}^{\sharp}):=\Sym(\Omega^{1}(\omega_{R}^{A,B,C}))\circ\left(\theta_{R}^{A,C}\right)^{-1}

which is seen visually as

\Sym\left(\Omega_{R/C}^{1}\right)\otimes_{C}A\xrightarrow{}\Sym\left(\Omega^{1}_{R\otimes_{C}A/A}\right)\xrightarrow{}\Sym\left(\Omega^{1}_{(R\otimes_{C}B)\otimes_{B}A}\right).

We then compute that on pure tensors
r\otimes a
for
r\in R
and
a\in A
,

\displaystyle\Gamma(\beta_{R}^{\sharp})(r\otimes a)

\displaystyle=\left(\Sym(\Omega^{1}(\omega_{R}^{A,B,C}))\circ\left(\theta_{R}^{A,C}\right)^{-1}\right)(r\otimes a)=\Sym(\Omega^{1}(\omega_{R}^{A,B,C}))(r\otimes a)

\displaystyle=(r\otimes 1_{B})\otimes a.

while for differentials pure tensors
\mathrm{d}r\otimes a
we get

\displaystyle\Gamma(\beta_{R}^{\sharp})(\mathrm{d}r\otimes a)

\displaystyle=\left(\Sym(\Omega^{1}(\omega_{R}^{A,B,C}))\circ\left(\theta_{R}^{A,C}\right)^{-1}\right)(\mathrm{d}r\otimes a)=\Sym(\Omega^{1}(\omega_{R}^{A,B,C}))\big(\mathrm{d}(r\otimes a)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\big((r\otimes 1_{B})\otimes a\big).

Because the pure tensors of the form
r\otimes a,\mathrm{d}r\otimes a
for
r\in R
and
a\in A
linearly generate
\Sym(\Omega_{R/C}^{1})\otimes_{C}A
, this completely determines the action of
\Gamma(\beta_{R}^{\sharp})
after extending linearly. Furthermore, because
\Gamma(\alpha_{R}^{\sharp})
and
\Gamma(\beta_{R}^{\sharp})
coincide on generators, it follows that they coincide and we get that
\Gamma(\alpha_{R}^{\sharp})=\Gamma(\beta_{R}^{\sharp})
. This implies that
\alpha_{R}=\beta_{R}
and so the two pasting diagrams are equivalent by once again invoking Lemma
6.8
. Furthermore, because
\Sch
is regarded as a
2
-category with trivial
2
-cells, the equivalence of these pasting diagrams implies that the data
(T_{-/X},\!\,_{f}{T}^{-1})
defines a pseudonatural transformation
T\colon\Sch_{/-}\Rightarrow\Sch_{/-}
.
∎

With all these facts, we now have the ingredients to define and construct the (equivariant) tangent structure on the equivariant category of schemes over a base variety
X
by way of the Zariski tangent indexing functor.

Definition 6.16
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a (quasi-projective) left
G
-variety. Define the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
\Scal
to be the one given in Item (11) of Example
5.19
, i.e.,
\Scal=(\Scal,\Sch_{/(-)})
is the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sf(G)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Scal}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sch_{/(-)}}

We then define the category of
descent equivariant schemes
16
16

16

The idea here is that the category
(\Sch_{/X})_{G}
describes all possible equivariant descent information as recorded by the actual schemes over
X
.
to be the equivariant category
(\Sch_{/X})_{G}:=\Scal_{G}(X)
. We also define the
descent equivariant Zariski tangent category on
X
to be the pseudolimit tangent category
((\Sch_{/X})_{G},\Tbb)
.

The fact that the definition of the descent equivariant Zariski tangent category is actually a tangent category is justified by the theorem below.

Theorem 6.17
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a (quasi-projective) left
G
-variety. Then the equivariant Zariski tangent category
((\Sch_{/X})_{G},\Tbb)
is a tangent category.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.33
it suffices to show that the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
\Sbb
is a tangent pre-equivariant indexing functor. However:

(1)

That each category
\Scal(\Gamma)
is a tangent category is Theorem
6.1
;

(2)

That each pair
(\Scal(f),\!\,_{f}{T})=(\of^{\ast},\!\,_{\of}{T})
is a strong tangent morphism is Proposition
6.14
;

(3)

That the data
(\!\,_{\Gamma}{T},\!\,_{f}{T}^{-1})=(T_{-/\XGamma},\!\,_{\of}{T^{-1}})
constitutes a pseudonatural transformation
T\colon\Scal\Rightarrow\Scal
follows from Proposition
6.15
.

Thus
(\Scal_{G}(X),\Tbb_{G})
is a tangent category and we are done.
∎

7.
A Sketch of the Descent Equivariant Tangent Structure on Smooth Manifolds

Perhaps unsurprisingly, we can play a similar game in the case of smooth manifolds as what we did with varieties above exactly because, as the variety case descends through smooth morphisms and smooth group resolutions, we are descending through principal
L
-bundles and the corresponding submersions in the manifold case. In order to sketch how to define a tangent category of descent equivariant smooth manifolds over a given left
L
-manifold
M
, we need an analogous result that the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
\Mcal=(\Mcal,\SMan_{/(-)})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FMan(L)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SMan_{\operatorname{sub}}^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times M}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mcal}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SMan_{/(-)}}

defines a tangent indexing functor. In this shorter section we sketch how to go about this by first recalling some general tangent category theory on tangent étale maps and giving a necessary and sufficient condition for a pullback functor between slice tangent categories to preserve the slice tangent functors (up to natural isomorphism, of course).

Definition 7.1

(
[
5
, Definition 35]
)
.

A morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
is
étale
if the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback diagram.

Example 7.2
.

The prototypical example of étale maps in a tangent category are the local diffeomorphisms in
\SMan
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
be a tangent category and let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism in a tangent category. Then if
f
is a tangent display map in the sense of
[
3
]
and if
f
is étale, then the morphism
\operatorname{pr}_{2}
in the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is tangent étale.

Proof.

Because the morphism
f
is a tangent display map, for any map
g\colon Z\to Y
the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

exists and is preserved by the tangent functor
T
. However, because
f
is étale this implies that
\operatorname{pr}_{2}
is étale by an application of
[
5
, Lemma 36]
.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
be a tangent category and let
f\colon X\to Y
be a tangent display morphism. Then for any morphism
g\colon Z\to Y
, the natural map

\theta\colon T(X\times_{Y}Z)\to X\times_{Y}T(Z)

is an isomorphism if and only if the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{2}\colon X\times_{Y}Z\to Z
is a tangent étale map.

Proof.

Recall that since
f
is a tangent display map, the pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}T(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{qr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{qr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

both exist. The natural map
\theta
is then constructed via the universal property of the pullback as presented in the diagram below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(X\times_{Y}Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}T(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\operatorname{pr}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ p_{X\times_{Y}Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{qr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{qr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Note that the map
\theta
is an isomorphism if and only if the cone morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(X\times_{Y}Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\operatorname{pr}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ p_{X\times_{Y}Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback diagram. However, this diagram factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(X\times_{Y}Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\operatorname{pr}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X\times_{Y}Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

so by the Pullback Stacking Lemma, the outer square is a pullback if and only if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(X\times_{Y}Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\operatorname{pr}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X\times_{Y}Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{2}}

is a pullback diagram. However, the diagram above is a pullback if and only if
\operatorname{pr}_{2}
is étale, giving the result.
∎

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be an étale tangent display map in a tangent category
\Cscr
. Then for any morphism
g\colon Z\to Y
there is an isomorphism

T(X\times_{Y}Z)\cong X\times_{Y}T(Z).

Proof.

Because
f
is a display morphism, it follows that the morphism
f\colon X\to Y
admits pullbacks against arbitrary morphisms
g\colon Z\to Y
and these pullback diagrams are all preserved by the tangent functor. Applying Lemma
7.3
gives that for any
g\colon Z\to Y
, the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{2}\colon X\times_{Y}Z\to Z
is tangent étale and so by Lemma
7.4
the natural map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\colon T(X\times_{Y}Z)\to X\times_{Y}T(Z)}

is an isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 7.6
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a local diffeomorphism in
\SMan
. Then for any morphism
g\colon Z\to Y
of smooth manifolds, there is an isomorphism

T\left(X\times_{Y}Z\right)\cong X\times_{Y}T(Z).

Proof.

Recall that local diffeomorphisms are exactly the morphisms in
\SMan
which are simultaneously immersions and submersions. Because local diffeomorphisms are étale in
\SMan
and submersions are tangent display maps in
\SMan
by
[
3
, Example 4.11.i]
, the result follows by applying Proposition
7.5
.
∎

Corollary 7.7
.

For any Lie group
L
satisfying Assumption
5.13
, for any left
L
-manifold
M
, and for any morphism
f\colon F\to E
in
\FMan(L)
, for any map
g\colon Z\to L\backslash(E\times M)
the natural morphism

\theta_{Z}\colon T\left(\big(L\backslash(F\times M)\big)\times_{L\backslash(E\times M)}Z\right)\longrightarrow\left(L\backslash\big(F\times M\big)\right)\times_{L\backslash(F\times M)}T(Z)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Note that since
\overline{f}\colon L\backslash(F\times M)\to L\backslash(E\times M)
is étale by Part (4) of Proposition
5.15
, we apply Corollary
7.6
to give the result.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

In what follows we’ll have to introduce slice categories with notationally complicated terms with many slash terms. In order to make sure terms are as readable as possible, we have adopted the comma category notation
\Cscr\downarrow X
in order to indicate the slice category of
\Cscr
over
X
.

Recall that by Part (4) of Proposition
5.15
, for each morphism
f\colon F\to E
in the category
\FMan(L)
, the corresponding morphism
\overline{f}\colon L\backslash(F\times M)\to L\backslash(E\times M)
is an étale map in
\SMan
. As such, because pullbacks against submersions (and hence étale maps) exist in
\SMan
, we have a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}^{\ast}\colon\SMan\downarrow{\big(L\backslash(E\times M)\big)}\to\SMan\downarrow{\big(L\backslash(F\times M)\big)}}

for every morphism in
\overline{f}
(which, in turn, is the technology which allows us to equip
\FMan(L)
with the pseudofunctor
\Mcal
described above). Our goal is to establish that this pseudofunctor is a tangent indexing functor when we regard each category
\SMan\downarrow X
as a tangent category with the slice tangent structure.

To establish this, observe that by Part (4) of Proposition
5.15
we know that
\overline{f}
is étale. Thus by Corollary
7.7
there is an isomorphism, for any smooth manifold
N\to L\backslash(E\times M)
,

\left(\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha}\right)_{N}\colon T\left(N\times_{L\backslash(E\times M)}\big(L\backslash(F\times M)\big)\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}TN\times_{L\backslash(E\times M)}\big(L\backslash(F\times M)\big).

That this ismorphism is natural in the structure map
N\to L\backslash(E\times M)
is trivial to verify by virtue of the universal property of pullbacks. We claim that the pairs

(\overline{f}^{\ast},\!\,_{f}{\alpha})\colon\SMan\downarrow{\big(L\backslash(E\times M)\big)}\longrightarrow\SMan\downarrow{\big(L\backslash(F\times M)\big)}

are strong tangent morphisms when each of the corresponding slice categories are given the slice tangent structures.

Proposition 7.9
.

For any Lie group
L
satisfying Assumption
5.13
, for any left
L
-manifold
M
, and for any morphism
f\colon F\to E
in
\FMan(L)
, the pair

(\overline{f}^{\ast},\!\,_{f}{\alpha})\colon\SMan\downarrow{\big(L\backslash(E\times M)\big)}\longrightarrow\SMan\downarrow{\big(L\backslash(F\times M)\big)}

constitutes a strong tangent morphism.

Proof.

Observe that because the functor
\overline{f}^{\ast}
is continuous, it restricts to a functor

\overline{f}^{\ast}\colon\mathbf{CMon}\left(\SMan\downarrow{\big(L\backslash(E\times M)\big)}\right)\longrightarrow\mathbf{CMon}\left(\SMan\downarrow{\big(L\backslash(F\times M)\big)}\right)

by virtue of preserving the relevant diagrams describing commutative monoids; moreover, by the fact that each map
\overline{f}
is a display map
\overline{f}^{\ast}
preserves the additivity of the tangent bundles and interacts appropriately with the bundle transformations
p,0,
and
\operatorname{add}
via
\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha}
and its pullback power
(\!\,_{f}{\alpha})_{2}
. Similarly,
\overline{f}^{\ast}
and
\!\,{}_{f}{\alpha}
interact appropriately with the lifts
\lambda
by virtue of the fact that
\overline{f}
is a tangent display map. The preservation of the canonical flip
c
comes down to verifying that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\backslash(F\times M)\times_{L\backslash(E\times M)}T^{2}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}\left(L\backslash(F\times M)\times_{L\backslash(E\times M)}N\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\backslash(F\times M)\times_{L\backslash(E\times M)}T^{2}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}\left(L\backslash(F\times M)\times_{L\backslash(E\times M)}N\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}^{\ast}\ast c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast\!\,_{f}{\alpha})\circ(\!\,_{f}{\alpha}\ast T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\ast\overline{f}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast\!\,_{f}{\alpha})\circ(\!\,_{f}{\alpha}\ast T)}

commutes, but this is a straightforward albeit tedious check using that each path around the square describes a cone morphism between the two isomorphic copies of the pullback.
∎

From here an extremely tedious but straightforward check shows that maps
(T,\!\,_{f}{\alpha}^{-1})
vary pseudofunctorially in
\FMan(L)^{\op}
; this essentially uses the fact that pullbacks vary pseudofunctorially and that the tangent functors themselves vary pseudonaturally over étale maps. Applying Theorem
3.33
shows that the category
\mathbf{PC}(\Mcal)=\Mcal_{L}(M)
is a tangent category of descent equivariant smooth manifolds over the manifold
M
. We collect these observations into a definition and theorem justifying the name below.

Definition 7.10
.

Let
L
be a Lie group satisfying Assumption
5.13
and let
M
be a left
L
-manifold. Then we define the category of
descent
L
-equivariant smooth manifolds over
M
to be the category

\Mcal_{L}(M)=:\left(\SMan_{/M}\right)_{L}

and we call the pseudolimit tangent structure the
descent
L
-equivariant tangent structure over
M
.

Theorem 7.11
.

The descent
L
-equivariant tangent structure on
M
claimed in Definition
7.10
exists.

Sketch.

In light of Theorem
3.33
we must argue that the pseudofunctor
\Mcal
is a tangent-indexing functor. However, this is argued by Proposition
7.9
and the discussion prior to the statement of Definition
7.10
.
∎

8.
Remarks Toward Future Work

In the last section of this paper we showed how to take the tangent categorical language of smooth manifolds and have it interact with the equivariant descent recorded by having a Lie group
L
act on a smooth manifold
M
. We are especially interested in examining this descent-equivariant tangent structure when
M
is an orbifold regarded as a
L
-space for some Lie group
L
(each orbifold can be represented this way; cf.
[
21
]
). In order to show that this is well-behaved for orbifolds, i.e. independent of the chosen presentation, we need to prove that different Morita equivalent representations of an orbifold give rise to suitably equivalent descent-equivariant (tangent) categories. This is left as a topic for future work, as it will involve delicate and technical arguments involving change of group functors and manipulations of bibundles.
